Rebooting quarter-BPS operators in 𝒩 = 𝟒 \boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}=4} Super Yang-Mills Preprint: UUITP-55/21
Agnese Bissi, Giulia Fardelli, and Andrea Manenti

Abstract
We start a systematic study of quarter-BPS operators in four-dimensional 𝒩 = 4 {\mathcal{N}=4} Super Yang-Mills with gauge group SU ⁡ ( N ) \mathrm{SU}(N) making use of recently developed tools in conformal field theory. We adapt the technology of embedding space tensor structures in four dimensions to the problem of computing R-symmetry tensor structures, and we use the underlying chiral algebra to obtain the superconformal Ward identities. This allows us to fix the protected part of the four-point correlators, up to few ambiguities. As applications, we use the Lorentzian inversion formula to study the leading order OPE data in the large N N supergravity limit and we make contact with the OPE limit of the five-point function of half-BPS operators.



Section 1
Introduction

In the last years, there has been a tremendous progress in studying four-point correlators of half-BPS operators in four dimensional Superconformal Field Theories (SCFTs) with conformal bootstrap techniques,
in particular in the context of
\mathcal{N}=4
supersymmetry, with the seminal papers
Beem:2013qxa
;
Beem:2016wfs
.
1
1

1

Several results have been obtained also in the context of
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetry, started in
Beem:2014zpa
.

The half-BPS superconformal primaries
\mathcal{O}_{p}
are scalar operators, of protected dimension
\Delta=p
, transforming under the
(0,p,0)
representation of the
\mathrm{SU}(4)
R-symmetry group. They are single trace operators made by the six scalar fields of
\mathcal{N}=4
super Yang-Mills.
The reason for these extensive studies is mainly twofold. These operators preserve the highest amount of supersymmetry, their conformal dimension as well as three-point correlators involving all such operators are protected, meaning that they are fixed by symmetries and do not contain dynamical information.
Moreover, higher-point correlators are strongly constrained by supersymmetry, both in their space-time and R-symmetry dependence. Remarkably, the existence of a chiral algebra, essentially linked to the
\mathcal{N}=2
superconformal invariance, has been shown to emerge when studying the meromorphic part of four-point correlators, thus this piece of information can be completely fixed by the free-field values for Super Yang-Mills (SYM) theories.
These properties have been crucial in applying conformal bootstrap techniques to these correlators, numerically and analytically, see the recent reviews
Poland:2018epd
;
Bissi:2021spj
.
From a different perspective, the study of these correlators is linked to amplitudes in the dual superstring theory description. In particular, the half-BPS operator with the lowest conformal dimension,
\Delta=2
, is the scalar component of the stress-energy supermultiplet, which is dual to the super-graviton multiplet. The entire tower of Kaluza-Klein modes of the graviton are instead dual to the
\Delta=p
with
p>2
half-BPS operators. Thus the study of these correlators using conformal bootstrap techniques, gives an operative method to explore super-graviton amplitudes on
\mathrm{AdS}
background, which have been inaccessible for several years, due to the increasing complications intrinsic to perturbative methods in this setup
Aharony:2016dwx
;
Alday:2017xua
;
Aprile:2017bgs
.

Driven by the conceptual advancements and the plethora of results obtained by studying this class of correlators, mostly in providing information on dynamical, coupling dependent quantities, in this paper we would like to revive the study of quarter-BPS operators in the context of
\mathcal{N}=4
Super Yang-Mills theories in four dimensions, with
\mathrm{SU}(N)
gauge group. These operators have protected conformal dimension, they transform in the
(q,p,q)
representation of the
\mathrm{SU}(4)
R-symmetry group and are a combination of a double trace and a single trace term, the latter being sub-leading in the large
N
expansion. These operators appear in all the context mentioned above, in particular they are present in the operator product expansion of half-BPS operators.

In this paper, we start the study of four-point correlators involving at least one quarter-BPS operator. We focus in particular on operators transforming under the representation
(2,0,2)
, since they are the first non-trivial ones and they appear in the operator product expansion of
\mathcal{O}_{2}\times\mathcal{O}_{2}
, making it possible to consider mixed correlator with such operators.
We exploit the power of the symmetries, using together the superconformal Ward identities and the underlying chiral algebra, to constrain, at least partially, the protected structure of such correlators. We computed free theory results and, by requiring the absence of higher-spin currents and other mild assumptions, we managed to partially resolve the ambiguities. In addition, using the inversion formula, we compute the sub-leading large-
N
correction to the CFT data, in the supergravity regime. We make contact with the recently computed five-point function of half-BPS operators of protected dimension two
Goncalves:2019znr
, by taking the OPE limit and projecting in the R-symmetry structure that we are interested in, and we found perfect agreement with all the checks that we made.

The paper is structured as follows. In section
2
we introduce the quarter-BPS operators, first from a representation theoretic point of view and then we specialize to the kinematics of their correlators. In section
3
we briefly review the chiral algebra construction in order to subsequently apply it to our correlators of interest. Then in section
4
we consider mixed correlators involving the
(2,0,2)
quarter-BPS operator
\mathcal{O}_{02}
, while in section
6
we present large-
N
results for the averaged anomalous dimensions of unprotected operators appearing in their OPE, after a review of the method presented in section
5
. In section
7
we prove that the correlator with one
\mathcal{O}_{02}
and three
\mathcal{O}_{2}
is protected at large
N
. Finally, section
8
contains some discussions and future directions. Several technical details are left to six appendices.

1.1
General idea

In this paper we focus on quarter-BPS operators
\mathcal{O}_{pq}
transforming in the
(q,p,q)
representation of the
\mathrm{SU}(4)
R-symmetry and which satisfy a shortening condition of the
\mathcal{B}
type — also known as
B\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
type.

When considering operators transforming in these representations, it is important to deal efficiently with all the R-symmetry indices that start appearing. To this aim, we introduce auxiliary null vectors, satisfying precise properties, contracting
\mathrm{SO}(6)
and
\mathrm{SU}(4)
(anti)fundamental indices

\mathcal{O}_{pq}(S,{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu},y)\equiv(\mathcal{O}_{pq})^{m_{1}\cdots m_{q}}_{n_{1}\cdots n_{q}\,,\,M_{1}\cdots M_{p}}\,{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{m_{1}}\cdots{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{m_{q}}\,S^{n_{1}}\cdots S^{n_{q}}\,y^{M_{1}}\cdots y^{M_{p}}\,.

(1.1)

This allows us to define all the tensor structures, now functions of products or combinations of these vectors. This is necessary in order to construct correlators of these operators as well as to analyze the different representations exchanged in the OPE.

When expanded in
\mathcal{N}=2
supermultiplets, the operators
\mathcal{O}_{pq}
contain half-BPS operators, which, according to the chiral algebra construction of
Beem:2013sza
, are of the Schur type. It is essentially this fact that allows us to identify a protected subsector in their correlation functions. More precisely, we are able to derive superconformal Ward identities for the four-point functions under analysis by imposing that the correlator should be meromorphic when computed in a special, space-time dependent, configuration of the
\mathrm{SU}(4)
polarizations, with the operator positions restricted to a plane. This configuration simultaneously selects the specific
\mathcal{N}=2
half-BPS operator inside
\mathcal{O}_{pq}
and performs the chiral algebra twist

\langle\mathcal{O}_{p_{1}q_{1}}\mathcal{O}_{p_{2}q_{2}}\mathcal{O}_{p_{3}q_{3}}\mathcal{O}_{p_{4}q_{4}}\rangle\quad\xrightarrow[\begin{subarray}{c}\hskip 8.19447pt\,y_{i}\cdot y_{j}\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\eta}_{i}\tilde{\eta}_{j}\,({\bar{z}}_{i}-{\bar{z}}_{j})\,,\hskip 8.19447pt\\
S_{i}\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{j}\,=\,{\bar{z}}_{i}-{\bar{z}}_{j}+\tilde{\eta}_{i}\tilde{\eta}_{j}\hskip 8.19447pt\end{subarray}]{}\quad\mathbb{T}(\tilde{\eta}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\eta}_{4})\,{\mathfrak{f}}(z)+\cdots

(1.2)

In the above schematic equation the
\tilde{\eta}_{i}
are some newly introduced
\mathrm{SU}(2)
polarizations that contract the flavor indices of the Schur operator inside
\mathcal{O}_{pq}
,
\mathbb{T}(\tilde{\eta}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\eta}_{4})
is a tensor structure
2
2

2

In principle, there could be more than one structure, but in our cases there will always be only one.
that scales as
\lambda^{p_{i}}
when
\tilde{\eta}_{i}\to\lambda\tilde{\eta}_{i}
and
z_{i},{\bar{z}}_{i}
are the positions on the chiral algebra plane of the four operators. The ellipsis denotes terms that scale differently in the
\tilde{\eta}_{i}
polarizations. The function
{\mathfrak{f}}(z)
is meromorphic in
z
and is coupling independent, so it can be computed from the free theory.

The basic idea behind these identities is that they provide a way to separate a correlator in a protected part, encoding the information deriving from the chiral algebra, from a set of unprotected and dynamical functions
\mathcal{H}_{m}
. Unfortunately this splitting is not unique: there exists always an ambiguity, namely a function that vanishes under the chiral algebra map, but it is not responsible for the exchange of unprotected multiplets. In this paper we fix this ambiguity as much as possible by imposing simple consistency requirements on the conformal block expansion of the correlator.

1.2
Summary of results

In order to illustrate the construction outlined above, we first apply it to the well-studied example of the correlator of four
\mathcal{O}_{2}
operators, reproducing the well-known results
Beem:2013qxa
;
Beem:2016wfs
.

Then we focus on four-point functions containing respectively one, two or four
\mathcal{O}_{02}
operators. Given the presence of Schur-type operators in the
\mathcal{N}=2
decomposition of
\mathcal{O}_{02}
, one could think of using
\mathcal{N}=2
superconformal blocks
Dolan:2001tt
;
Beem:2014zpa
to study these correlators. Unfortunately, the
\mathcal{N}=2
half-BPS operator in
\mathcal{O}_{02}
comes together with long multiplets, for whose correlators an expansion in superconformal blocks is not known yet.

In analysing the various four-point functions, we start by fixing the protected part as much as possible, then we extract corrections at large
N
to the OPE data of operators in the OPE
\mathcal{O}_{2}\times\mathcal{O}_{02}
and
\mathcal{O}_{02}\times\mathcal{O}_{02}
by means of the Lorentzian inversion formula. We cannot yet compute the anomalous dimensions of the single eigenstates due to the inevitable mixing of degenerate operators that will take place even at tree level. For this reason we only quote the results for the averaged quantities

\langle a^{(0)}\,\gamma^{(1)}\rangle=\sum_{I}a^{(0)}_{\mathcal{O}_{I}}\,\gamma^{(1)}_{\mathcal{O}_{I}}\,,\qquad\langle a^{(1)}\rangle=\sum_{I}a^{(1)}_{\mathcal{O}_{I}}\,,

(1.3)

where
\gamma^{(1)}
is the first correction to the conformal dimension,
a^{(i)}
is the
i
-th order OPE coefficient squared and the sum is over all operators with same bare dimension.

By taking the OPE limit of the supergravity five-point function of all
\mathcal{O}_{2}
in
Goncalves:2019znr
, we prove that
\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
is protected. This result was not known in the literature as far as we are aware.
3
3

3

Although
Goncalves:2019znr
argued for some OPE coefficient in their block expansion to be protected.
In doing this analysis, we develop a machinery to deal with five-point tensor structures and to project them to four-point ones that can be easily generalized to higher-points.

Section 2
Generalities of quarter-BPS operators

2.1
Superconformal representation theory

Studying correlators of quarter-BPS scalar primaries involves various technical challenges, mainly due to the rapid growth of R-symmetry tensor structures. In this section, however, we would like to introduce the operators of interest from a purely representation theoretic point of view, keeping the technical details to a minimum. The quarter-BPS operator
\mathcal{O}_{pq}
, for
q>0
, is defined to be the superconformal primary of the following multiplet

B\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[0\hskip 0.5pt;0]^{(q,p,q)}_{2q+p}\,,\qquad\text{or}\qquad\mathcal{B}_{[q,p,q](0,0)}\,,

(2.1)

where in the left we use the notation of
Cordova:2016emh
and in the right that of
Dolan:2002zh
. We will use the former for the rest of the paper. When
q>0
the operator satisfies a quarter-BPS shortening condition because it is annihilated by four supercharges, namely

Q^{(1,0,0)}_{+}\,,\qquad{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{(0,0,1)}_{\dot{+}}\,,\qquad Q^{(1,0,0)}_{-}\,,\qquad{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{(0,0,1)}_{\dot{-}}\,,

(2.2)

where the superscript denotes the
\mathrm{SU}(4)
representation and the subscript the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
spin.
4
4

4

If instead
q=0
the superconformal primary is annihilated by two additional supercharges, namely
Q^{(-1,1,0)}_{+}
and
{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{(0,1,-1)}_{\dot{+}}
,
consequently, the shortening condition becomes half-BPS and indeed it yields the familiar multiplet
\mathcal{O}_{p}
.

Naively, the simplest such multiplet would be
\mathcal{O}_{01}
, which transforms in the
(1,0,1)
of
\mathrm{SU}(4)
and has dimension two. However this operator only appears in free theories because it has a higher spin conserved current at level six

Q^{3}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}{}^{3}\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{O}_{01}\sim[3\hskip 1.0pt;\hskip-0.8pt3]^{(0,0,0)}_{5}\,.

(2.3)

Moreover, given its conformal dimension, it must be built out of two fundamental fields
\varphi^{M}_{I}(x)
with the gauge index contracted and the
\mathrm{SU}(4)
index antisymmetrized.
5
5

5

Refer to table
9
in appendix
A
for the naming of the indices.
This contraction, without any additional indices, is vanishing. The next simplest operators are
\mathcal{O}_{11}
and
\mathcal{O}_{21}
, which have dimensions
3
and
4
respectively. In this case, it can be shown that the trace over
\mathrm{SU}(N)
vanishes and therefore no such operators can be constructed
DHoker:2003csh
.
6
6

6

This is proven in detail in appendix
C
for the interested reader.

Thus, the first non trivial operator that one can consider is
\mathcal{O}_{02}
, which has dimension four and transforms in the
(2,0,2)
representation of
\mathrm{SU}(4)
.

The superprimary of each of these multiplets can be built out of the six scalars
\varphi^{M}_{I}(x)
. As it is well known, the representations of the type
(0,p,0)
are obtained by taking a symmetric traceless combination of the
\mathrm{SO}(6)
indices
M_{i}
, as follows

\mathcal{O}_{p}=\tr\hskip 1.0pt(T^{I_{1}}\cdots T^{I_{p}})\,\varphi^{(M_{1}}_{I_{1}}\cdots\varphi^{M_{p})}_{I_{p}}-\mbox{traces}\,.

(2.4)

While the representations
(q,0,q)
are built by antisymmetrizing the indices in pairs. More precisely, we can make use of the six-dimensional rotation matrices
(\Sigma^{MN})_{m}^{\phantom{m}n}
,
({\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}{}^{MN})^{m}_{\phantom{m}n}
, defined in appendix
A
. These operators normally are a linear combination of single and double traces
Ryzhov:2001bp
;
DHoker:2003csh
. The coefficients of the linear combination are fixed by imposing that the two-point function is unit-normalized and the operator is a short superconformal primary. If, for the moment, we leave aside the
\mathrm{SU}(N)
part, we can write

\mathcal{O}_{0q}=\tr\hskip 1.0pt(T^{I_{1}}\cdots)\,\tr\hskip 1.0pt(\cdots T^{I_{2q}})\,\varphi^{M_{1}}_{I_{1}}\cdots\varphi^{M_{2q}}_{I_{2q}}\,(\Sigma_{M_{1}M_{2}})_{m_{1}}^{\phantom{m_{1}}{n_{1}}}\cdots(\Sigma_{M_{2q-1}M_{2q}})_{m_{q}}^{\phantom{m_{q}}{n_{q}}}\,.

(2.5)

The detailed expression will be given only for the operator under study,
\mathcal{O}_{02}
. General operators
\mathcal{O}_{pq}
with both labels nonzero are obtained with a combination of the two index contractions presented above.

In section
3
we will make use of the chiral algebra of
Beem:2013sza
for finding the superconformal Ward identities satisfied by the four-point functions of
\mathcal{O}_{pq}
. It is therefore beneficial to learn how to expand these multiplets into
\mathcal{N}=2
submultiplets, since the chiral algebra is an
\mathcal{N}=2
construction. In particular, we want to look for operators which satisfy two equalities between the conformal dimension
\Delta
, the spin
j,{\bar{\jmath}}
, the
\mathrm{U}(1)_{R}
R-charge
r
and the
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{R}
R-charge
7
7

7

Note that our convention is to denote representations by their Dynkin labels, so spin-
\frac{1}{2}
would be
R=1
.

R

r=j-{\bar{\jmath}}\,,\qquad 2\Delta=j+{\bar{\jmath}}+2R\,.

(2.6)

These are termed Schur operators. The quantum numbers
r
and
R
arise from the familiar breaking of the
\mathrm{SU}(4)_{R}
R-symmetry group into

\mathrm{SU}(4)_{R}\;\longrightarrow\;\mathrm{SU}(2)_{R}\times\mathrm{U}(1)_{R}\times\mathrm{SU}(2)_{F}\,.

(2.7)

The equalities (
2.6
) define a superconformal primary only if
r=j=0
. In this case the operator belongs to an
\mathcal{N}=2
half-BPS multiplet. At level zero of
\mathcal{O}_{pq}
we find precisely these multiplets, with a multiplicity of
p+1

B\bar{B}[0\hskip 0.5pt;\hskip-0.8pt0]^{(q,p,q)}_{2q+p}\quad\supset\quad(p+1)\,B\bar{B}[0\hskip 0.5pt;\hskip-0.8pt0]^{(2q+p;\hskip 0.5pt0)}_{2q+p}\,.

(2.8)

The multiplicity implies that the Schur operator extracted from
\mathcal{O}_{pq}
is also transforming in the charge-
p
representation of the flavor group
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{F}
which appears in (
2.7
). So, to summarize, the quarter-BPS operator
\mathcal{O}_{pq}
contains, at level zero, an
\mathcal{N}=2
half-BPS primary operator which has
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{R}
R-charge equal to
2q+p
and flavor charge equal to
p
. This is the operator that we will exploit in order to derive the superconformal Ward identities.

2.2
Protected three-point functions

In this section, we collect known facts about three-point functions involving quarter-BPS operators.
In
DHoker:2001jzy
, the authors analyze three-point functions including quarter-BPS operators in a weak coupling expansions and they find that certain classes of correlators are protected at order
g^{2}
for any value of
N
based on SU(4), SU(
N
) arguments and explicit space-time computations. These are

1.

\langle\mathcal{O}_{p}\mathcal{O}_{q}\mathcal{O}\rangle
, where
\mathcal{O}
is a
\frac{1}{2}
-,
\frac{1}{4}
- or
\frac{1}{8}
-BPS operator.

2.

\langle\mathcal{O}_{pq}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5mu\mathcal{O}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{rs}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
either vanish because
(q,p,q)\not\in(s,r,s)\otimes(0,2,0)
or can be related to

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{pq}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5mu\mathcal{O}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{(p-2)q}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle\,,\quad\langle\mathcal{O}_{pq}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5mu\mathcal{O}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{(p+2)(q-2)}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle\,,\quad\langle\mathcal{O}_{pq}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5mu\mathcal{O}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{p(q-2)}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle\,,

(2.9a)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{pq}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5mu\mathcal{O}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{(p+2)(q-1)}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle\,,\quad\langle\mathcal{O}_{pq}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5mu\mathcal{O}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{pq}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle\,.

(2.9b)

The three-point functions (
2.9a
) turn out to be protected since they are extremal, namely that the dimension of one operator is equal to the sum of the remaining ones. Then, the first one in (
2.9b
) can be proved to vanish based on a SU
(N)
and SU(4) reasoning. Finally the last three-point function can be shown to be protected through more complicated arguments.

3.

Generic
\langle\mathcal{O}_{pq}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5mu\mathcal{O}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{rs}\mathcal{O}_{k}\rangle
are protected at order
g^{2}
if one of the following conditions is satisfied

\displaystyle\begin{cases}2s+r=2q+p+k\\
2q+p=2s+r+k\\
2q+p+2s+r=k\\
\end{cases}

extremality condition ,

(2.10a)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}2s+r\leq k+p\\
2q+p\leq k+r\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ ``three flavours'' condition}\,,

(2.10b)

\displaystyle k\leq p+r

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ ``two flavours'' condition}\,.

(2.10c)

Specializing to operators with scaling dimension
\Delta\leq 7
, the only three-point function not included in the previous cases is
\langle\mathcal{O}_{13}\mathcal{O}_{13}\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle
, with
\mathcal{O}_{13}
as defined in
Ryzhov:2001bp
. This is proved to be protected as well.

4.

\langle\mathcal{O}_{pq}\mathcal{O}_{rs}\mathcal{O}_{lk}\rangle
with
2k+l\leq 2q+p\leq 2s+r
are guaranteed to not receive
g^{2}
corrections if they satisfy either the extremality condition

2s+r=2q+p+2k+l

(2.11)

or all of the “three flavours” conditions

\displaystyle 2s+r

\displaystyle\leq 2k+l+p\,,

(2.12)

\displaystyle 2s+r

\displaystyle\leq 2q+p+l\,,

\displaystyle 2q+p

\displaystyle\leq 2k+l+s\,.

Notice that these conditions are for instance satisfied by
\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{02}\rangle
. Then among quarter-BPS operators with dimension less than seven, the only three-point functions left are

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{22}\rangle\,,\quad\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{12}\mathcal{O}_{32}\rangle\,,\quad\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{12}\mathcal{O}_{13}\rangle\,,

(2.13)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{13}\mathcal{O}_{32}\rangle\,,\quad\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{13}\mathcal{O}_{14}\rangle\,,

(2.14)

which are shown to be protected.

Similar analyses can be found in
Heslop:2003xu
;
Heslop:2001gp
.

A parallel and complementary study of three-point functions can be done resorting to the underlying chiral algebra.
8
8

8

We thank Xinan Zhou for suggesting this approach to us.
The idea is that if, in a given three-point function, it appears only one structure that survives to the chiral algebra map, this directly implies that the correlator is protected in the full
\mathcal{N}=4
theory. We have performed this analysis for the mixed three-point functions of
\mathcal{O}_{2}
and
\mathcal{O}_{02}
and for
\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{02}\rangle
. Proceeding in this way, we confirmed the protected nature of
\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
. Unfortunately, for the remaining two three-point functions the chiral algebra argument is not that constraining. We find that in
\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
, the chiral map kills the only SU(4) structure not giving us any new insight. The case of all
\mathcal{O}_{02}
operators is even different. Here, it turns out that a particular linear combination of the appearing tensor structures is indeed protected, but this is not enough to conclude that the entire function does not get renormalized as the weak-coupling analysis is suggesting. It seems that the conditions imposed by the chiral algebra are weaker than the ones outlined above. One of the reason can hinge on the fact that, differently from the
\mathcal{O}_{p}
case, when decomposed in
\mathcal{N}=2
supermultiplets,
\mathcal{O}_{02}
does not contain only a Schur operator but other non protected ones. The presence of this additional operators can possibly justify why the chiral algebra is insufficient or less powerful in studying correlators containing this quarter-BPS operators.

2.3
R-symmetry structures

2.3.1
Polarizations for
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}=4}

Due to the high proliferation of indices and the complicated symmetrizations and subtractions that one needs to do in order to construct quarter-BPS operators, it is convenient to introduce an index-free formalism for
\mathrm{SU}(4)
structures.

The same idea was used in
Nirschl:2004pa
to greatly simplify the computation of tensor structures, and has also been applied in the context of spinning operators
Costa:2011mg
;
Elkhidir:2014woa
;
Cuomo:2017wme
. In fact, the embedding formalism in four dimensions differs from our setup simply by a signature:
\mathrm{SU}(2,2)
versus
\mathrm{SU}(4)
.
9
9

9

Another obvious difference with the spinning structure formalism is that the six-dimensional vectors there are positions, so they can appear in the denominator. Here they are polarizations so they cannot.

We contract all
\mathrm{SO}(6)
fundamental indices with a six-dimensional complex vector
y^{M}
and all
\mathrm{SU}(4)
(anti)fundamental indices with a four-vector
S^{m}
(
{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{m}
). One can trade an
\mathrm{SO}(6)
index with an antisymmetrized pair of
\mathrm{SU}(4)
indices using the Dirac matrices defined in appendix
A
, thus we can also define

{\mathrm{y}}_{mn}\equiv y_{M}\Sigma^{M}_{mn}\,,\qquad\bar{{\mathrm{y}}}^{mn}\equiv y_{M}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}{}^{M\hskip 1.0ptmn}\,.

These polarizations must satisfy some constraints following from the irreducibility of the representation to which they are attached. The list of constraints reads

y\cdot y=0\,,\qquad S\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=0\,,\qquad{\mathrm{y}}\hskip 1.0ptS=0\,,\qquad\bar{\mathrm{y}}\hskip 1.0pt{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=0\,.

(2.15)

An operator in the
(q,p,q)
will be a field with homogeneity
p
in
y
and
q
in
S,{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}

\mathcal{O}_{pq}(\lambda S,\bar{\lambda}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu},\mu y)=(\lambda\bar{\lambda})^{q}\mu^{p}\,\mathcal{O}_{pq}(S,{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu},y)\,.

(2.16)

In order to recover the tensor form of this operator we must differentiate with respect to the polarizations. However, we need to be careful because the polarizations are constrained and so their derivatives are not free. This problem can be solved by defining differential operators which are interior to the constraints (
2.15
). With the aid of those operators we can recover

(\mathcal{O}_{pq})^{m_{1}\cdots m_{q}}_{n_{1}\cdots n_{q}\,,\,M_{1}\cdots M_{p}}=\partial_{n_{1}}\cdots\partial_{n_{q}}\,\bar{\partial}^{m_{1}}\cdots\bar{\partial}^{m_{q}}\,\mathcal{D}_{M_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{D}_{M_{p}}\mathcal{O}_{pq}(S,{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu},y)\,.

(2.17)

In (
A.7
) we show an explicit definition of
\mathcal{D}_{M}
,
\partial_{m}
and
\bar{\partial}^{m}
, which previously appeared in
Bargmann:1977gy
;
Nirschl:2004pa
;
Costa:2011mg
;
Cuomo:2017wme
. This definition solves only the first two constraints and therefore can be used only when either
p
or
q
is zero, which is enough for our cases.
10
10

10

In principle there are also unconstrained parametrizations of
y
and
S,{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}

Simmons-Duffin:2012juh
. Since this is not strictly necessary for our goals we have chosen not to pursue this direction.

The various polarizations can be contracted into index-free expressions which then combine to give the
n
-point tensor structures. A complete basis of such building blocks is
11
11

11

For six or more points, one also has to consider the Levi-Civita tensor
\epsilon_{MNPQRS}\,y_{i}^{M}y_{j}^{N}y_{k}^{P}y_{l}^{Q}y_{m}^{R}y_{n}^{S}
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{y}_{ij}

\displaystyle=y_{i}\cdot y_{j}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{ij}

\displaystyle=S_{i}\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{j}\,,

(2.18)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Y}_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}\cdots}

\displaystyle=\tr\hskip 1.0pt({\mathrm{y}}_{i_{1}}\bar{\mathrm{y}}_{i_{2}}{\mathrm{y}}_{i_{3}}\cdots)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\mathsf{J}^{ik}_{j_{1}\cdots j_{2p}}

\displaystyle=S_{i}\hskip 1.0pt{\mathrm{y}}_{j_{1}}\hskip-0.8pt\cdots\hskip 1.0pt\bar{{\mathrm{y}}}_{j_{2p}}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{k}\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}^{ik}_{j_{1}\cdots j_{2p+1}}

\displaystyle=S_{i}\hskip 1.0pt{\mathrm{y}}_{j_{1}}\hskip-0.8pt\cdots\hskip 1.0pt{\mathrm{y}}_{j_{2p+1}}S_{k}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{K}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{ik}_{j_{1}\cdots j_{2p+1}}

\displaystyle={\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{i}\hskip 1.0pt\bar{\mathrm{y}}_{j_{1}}\hskip-0.8pt\cdots\hskip 1.0pt\bar{{\mathrm{y}}}_{j_{2p+1}}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{k}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{ijkl}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{mnpq}\hskip 1.0ptS_{i}^{m}S_{j}^{n}S_{k}^{p}S_{l}^{q}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5mu\mathcal{E}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{ijkl}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{mnpq}\hskip 1.0pt{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{im}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{jn}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{kp}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{lq}\,,

With this notation, the most general two-point function of an operator
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{(q,p,{\bar{q}})}
of dimension
\Delta
transforming in the
(q,p,{\bar{q}})
of
\mathrm{SU}(4)
and its conjugate can be written as follows:

\langle\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{(q,p,{\bar{q}})}(x_{1})\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{({\bar{q}},p,q)}(x_{2})\rangle=\frac{(\mathsf{y}_{12})^{p}(\mathsf{S}_{12})^{q}(\mathsf{S}_{21})^{{\bar{q}}}}{(x_{12}^{2})^{\Delta}}\,.

(2.19)

2.3.2
Polarizations for
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}=2}

We also need a formalism for dealing with R-symmetry and flavor indices of
\mathcal{N}=2
tensor structures. This is again analogous to four dimensions, but in position space. Indeed, we have
\mathfrak{su}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{su}(2)
which differs from
\mathfrak{so}(3,1)
by the signature.

We contract all R-symmetry indices with a complex two-vector
\eta^{a}
and all flavor indices with another two-vector
\tilde{\eta}{}^{a^{\prime}}
. Indices are raised and lowered with the Levi-Civita tensors
\epsilon_{ab}
and
\epsilon_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}
. The only possible building blocks are

\eta_{ij}=\eta_{i}^{a}\eta_{j\hskip 1.0pta}\,,\qquad\tilde{\eta}_{ij}=\tilde{\eta}_{i\hskip 1.0pta^{\prime}}\tilde{\eta}_{j}^{a^{\prime}}\,.

(2.20)

Clearly, due to the antisymmetry of
\epsilon
, the above expressions are nonzero for
i\neq j
. For this reason, we do not need to put further constraints on these vectors and there are no issues in taking derivatives. The two-point functions are also easy to write down. Given an
\mathcal{N}=2
primary
O_{R,F}
with R-charge
R
, flavor charge
F
and dimension
\Delta
one has

\langle O_{R,F}(x_{1})\hskip 1.0pt\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muO\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{R,F}(x_{2})\rangle=\frac{(\eta_{12})^{R}\,(\tilde{\eta}_{12})^{F}}{(x_{12}^{2})^{\Delta}}\,.

(2.21)

2.3.3
Tensor structures as Casimir eigenvectors

Let us consider a four-point function of four quarter-BPS operators

G(p_{1},q_{1};\hskip 1.0pt\ldots;\hskip 1.0ptp_{4},q_{4})=\langle\mathcal{O}_{p_{1}q_{1}}(x_{1},{\mathbf{S}}_{1})\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{O}_{p_{2}q_{2}}(x_{2},{\mathbf{S}}_{2})\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{O}_{p_{3}q_{3}}(x_{3},{\mathbf{S}}_{3})\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{O}_{p_{4}q_{4}}(x_{4},{\mathbf{S}}_{4})\rangle\,,

(2.22)

where
{\mathbf{S}}_{i}
collectively denotes
S_{i},{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{i}
and
y_{i}
. This correlator can be expanded into a certain number
N_{\mathrm{str}}
of functions. Each of these functions is multiplied by a combination of the polarizations defined previously, which we call
\mathbb{T}_{k}
, where each
k
is associated to a given representation exchanged. The allowed representations are contained in the following tensor product

\mathcal{R}\in(q_{1},p_{1},q_{1})\otimes(q_{2},p_{2},q_{2})\;\cap\;(q_{3},p_{3},q_{3})\otimes(q_{4},p_{4},q_{4})\,.

(2.23)

We can therefore write the correlator as

G(p_{1},q_{1};\hskip 1.0pt\ldots;\hskip 1.0ptp_{4},q_{4})={\hyperlink{eqref:kinPref}{\mathcal{K}_{2q_{1}+p_{1},\,\ldots,\,2q_{4}+p_{4}}}}\hskip 1.0pt\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{str}}}\mathbb{T}_{k}\,g_{k}(z,{\bar{z}})\,.

(2.24)

Above we defined the four-point prefactor
K
and the cross-ratios
z,{\bar{z}}
, as follows

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\hyperlink{eqref:kinPref}{\mathcal{K}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}}}=\frac{\left(\frac{x_{24}^{2}}{x_{14}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{12}}\left(\frac{x_{14}^{2}}{x_{13}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{34}}}{(x_{12}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2})}(x_{34}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4})}}\,,\qquad\Delta_{i}=2q_{i}+p_{i}\,,\quad\Delta_{ij}=\Delta_{i}-\Delta_{j}\,,

(2.25)

\displaystyle\frac{x_{12}^{2}\hskip 1.0ptx_{34}^{2}}{x_{13}^{2}\hskip 1.0ptx_{24}^{2}}=u=z{\bar{z}}\,,\qquad\frac{x_{14}^{2}\hskip 1.0ptx_{23}^{2}}{x_{13}^{2}\hskip 1.0ptx_{24}^{2}}=v=(1-z)(1-{\bar{z}})\,.

One way to find the tensor structures
\mathbb{T}_{k}
is to first write down a basis using all possible products of the monomials in (
2.18
), while making sure that they satisfy the scaling (
2.16
) for all four operators, and then to rotate this basis to one that diagonalizes the
\mathrm{SU}(4)
Casimir operators. The eigenvalues associated to each tensor structure will tell us the representation to which they correspond. In our cases of interest, the quadratic Casimir is not enough because different representations can have the same eigenvalue. It is therefore necessary to consider one higher order Casimir as well and for simplicity we use the quartic one instead of the cubic.
12
12

12

The reason is that the cubic Casimir contains an
\epsilon
tensor which makes the index contractions more involved.

Any operator in the
\mathrm{SU}(4)
universal enveloping algebra can be represented in terms of differential operators acting on the polarizations
S_{i},{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{i}
and
y_{i}
. Concretely, the three Casimirs of
\mathrm{SU}(4)
can be represented as
Karateev:2017yoq

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2}(\partial_{{\mathbf{S}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.0ptL_{MN}\hskip 1.0ptL^{NM}\,,

(2.26)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{3}(\partial_{{\mathbf{S}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24i}\hskip 1.0pt\epsilon^{MNPQRS}\hskip 1.0ptL_{MN}\hskip 1.0ptL_{PQ}\hskip 1.0ptL_{RS}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{4}(\partial_{{\mathbf{S}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.0ptL_{MN}\hskip 1.0ptL^{NP}\hskip 1.0ptL_{PQ}\hskip 1.0ptL^{QM}\,,

where
L_{MN}
are the generators of
\mathrm{SU}(4)
. Since we want to act on the first two points, their expression is

\displaystyle L_{MN}

\displaystyle=L_{1,\,MN}+L_{2,\,MN}\,,

(2.27)

\displaystyle L_{i,\,MN}

\displaystyle=-\left(y_{iM}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{N}_{i}}-y_{iN}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{M}_{i}}\right)-S^{m}_{i}\hskip 1.0pt\Sigma_{MN}{}_{m}^{\phantom{m}n}\frac{\partial}{\partial S^{n}_{i}}-{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{im}\hskip 1.0pt\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{MN}{}^{m}_{\phantom{m}n}\frac{\partial}{\partial{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{in}}\,.

If
\mathbb{T}_{k}
exchanges the representation
(q,p,{\bar{q}})
then it must satisfy the following eigenvalue equations

\mathcal{C}_{r}(\partial_{{\mathbf{S}}_{1}},\partial_{{\mathbf{S}}_{2}})\,\mathbb{T}_{k}({\mathbf{S}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{S}}_{4})=C_{r}\,\mathbb{T}_{k}({\mathbf{S}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{S}}_{4})\,,\qquad r=2,4\,,

(2.28)

with the eigenvalues given by
Perelomov2:1966

\displaystyle C_{2}

\displaystyle=p\left({\bar{q}}+q+4\right)+\frac{1}{4}\left(3{\bar{q}}^{2}+2(q+6){\bar{q}}+3q(q+4)\right)+p^{2}\,,

(2.29)

\displaystyle C_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}\big(\left({\bar{q}}+2p+q\right)^{2}\left({\bar{q}}+2p+q+8\right)^{2}+24\left({\bar{q}}+2p+q\right)\left({\bar{q}}+2p+q+8\right)

\displaystyle-2\left(q-{\bar{q}}\right)^{2}\left({\bar{q}}+q+2\right)^{2}+4\left({\bar{q}}\left({\bar{q}}+2\right)+q(q+2)\right)^{2}\big)\,.

For this work, we implemented the Casimir differential operators in a
Mathematica
notebook which can be made available upon request.

2.4
Relation with older superspace formulations

Quarter-BPS operators in
\mathcal{N}=4
Super Yang-Mills have been studied in detail since the early 2000s
Ryzhov:2001bp
;
DHoker:2003csh
. In this subsection we will show how to connect our formalism to the approach adopted in the past which made use of superspace.

There are various families of superspaces for
\penalty{\mathcal{N}=4}
. Any given superspace formulation is designed to make the shortening conditions appear “simple.” This means that imposing that a certain operator is short simply amounts to declaring that it depends on only a subset of the superspace coordinates. The most familiar example is a chiral multiplet in a four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=1
theory. Without a formulation of chiral superspace one would have to introduce a field
\mathcal{O}(x,\theta,{\bar{\theta}})
and require
{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\alpha}\mathcal{O}(x,\theta,{\bar{\theta}})=0
. But if we define
y=x+i\theta\sigma{\bar{\theta}}
then it suffices to take an arbitrary (unconstrained) field
\mathcal{O}(y,\theta)
and it is going to be automatically chiral. Generalizing this idea to extended supersymmetry is the main challenge that has been undertaken in the early nineties and that we wish to briefly review now.

The family of superspaces that we wish to review was introduced in
Hartwell:1994rp
and it takes the name of
(\mathcal{N},\mathrm{p},\mathrm{q})
superspace. The construction roughly goes as follows: we start with complexified super Minkowski space
\mathbb{C}^{4|4\mathcal{N}}
on which we require that the
x^{\mu}
coordinates are real and
{\bar{\theta}}_{m{\dot{\alpha}}}=(\theta^{m}_{\alpha})^{*}
. Let us call this space
M_{\mathcal{N}}
. Next we enlarge this space by a “flag manifold”
\mathbb{F}_{\mathrm{p},\mathrm{q}}
defined as the coset space

\mathbb{F}_{\mathrm{p},\mathrm{q}}\equiv\frac{\mathrm{SU}(\mathcal{N})}{\mathrm{S}(\mathrm{U}(\mathrm{p})\times\mathrm{U}(\mathcal{N}-\mathrm{p}-\mathrm{q})\times\mathrm{U}(\mathrm{q}))}\,.

(2.30)

This coset is parametrized by a special unitary matrix
u_{R}^{\phantom{R}m}
such that the index
m
is acted upon by the full
\mathrm{SU}(\mathcal{N})
group and the index
R
by the isotropy group — i.e. the group at the denominator of (
2.30
). All in all, this means that the coordinates of our superspace are
(x^{\mu},\theta^{m}_{\alpha},{\bar{\theta}}_{m{\dot{\alpha}}},u_{R}^{\phantom{R}m})
.

Superfields carry a representation of the so-called Levi subalgebra
13
13

13

The origin of this comes from interpreting the full superspace as a coset. This in turn follows from interpreting
M_{\mathcal{N}}
as a coset of
\mathrm{SU}(2,2|\mathcal{N})
.

\mathfrak{l}=\mathfrak{sl}(2|\mathrm{p})\oplus\mathfrak{sl}(2|\mathrm{q})\oplus\mathfrak{sl}(\mathcal{N}-\mathrm{p}-\mathrm{q})\oplus\mathbb{C}^{2}\,.

(2.31)

The bosonic parts of the first two summands and the last are the spin labels
j,{\bar{\jmath}}
. The two complex dimensions are
\Delta
and the
r
-charge — which for
\mathcal{N}=4
disappears and
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is replaced by
\mathbb{C}
— and the remaining parts are R-symmetry quantum numbers. Let us call this representation
\mathcal{R}
with carrier space
V_{\mathcal{R}}
. Then superfields are defined as sections of a
V_{\mathcal{R}}
bundle over
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{N}}\equiv M_{\mathcal{N}}\times\mathbb{F}_{\mathrm{p},\mathrm{q}}
. Equivalently, they are functions from
\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{N}}
to
V_{\mathcal{R}}
that satisfy an equivariance property

\displaystyle f:\mathbb{M}_{\mathcal{N}}

\displaystyle\to V_{\mathcal{R}}\,,

(2.32)

\displaystyle f(gx)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}(g)f(x)\qquad\forall\;g\in\exp(\mathfrak{l})\,.

In order to impose shortening conditions it is necessary to require that certain covariant derivatives annihilate the superfield. The whole point of this construction is that, for the fields of interest, requiring
Df=0
amounts to an holomorphicity constraint. So we do not need to deal with constrained superfields but we simply have superfields that depend on a subset of variables. There are two notions of holomorphicity: G-analiticity and H-analiticity. A superfield which is both G-analytic and H-analytic is called CR-analytic or simply analytic. A field is G-analytic if it satisfies
14
14

14

See e.g.
Hartwell:1994rp
for the explicit definitions of the derivatives.

u_{r}^{\phantom{r}m}D_{\alpha m}\,f={\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{m}(u^{\dagger})_{m}^{\phantom{m}r^{\prime}}\,f=0\,,

(2.33)

where
D_{\alpha i}
and
{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{i}
are the covariant derivatives on
M_{\mathcal{N}}
, the index
r
is on
\mathrm{SU}(\mathrm{p})
and the index
r^{\prime}
is on
\mathrm{SU}(\mathrm{q})
. Next one defines left-invariant vector fields of
\mathrm{SU}(\mathcal{N})
,
D_{R}^{\phantom{R}S}
, and splits the indices
R=(r,r^{\prime\prime},r^{\prime})
with
r,r^{\prime}
as before and
r^{\prime\prime}
being on
\mathrm{SU}(\mathcal{N}-\mathrm{p}-\mathrm{q})
. With this definition a field is H-analytic if it satisfies

D_{r}^{\phantom{r}s^{\prime}}\,f=D_{r}^{\phantom{r}s^{\prime\prime}}\,f=D_{r^{\prime\prime}}^{\phantom{r^{\prime\prime}}s^{\prime}}\,f=0\,.

(2.34)

The operator of
B_{1}\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}
type, namely half-BPS
(0,p,0)
or quarter-BPS
(q,p,q)
, are realized as CR-analytic superfields.

Let us fix
\mathcal{N}=4
and take
\mathrm{p}
and
\mathrm{q}
as in
DHoker:2003csh
, namely
\mathrm{p}=\mathrm{q}=1
. By looking at (
2.31
) we see that, other than the usual conformal quantum numbers, we have an extra
\mathfrak{su}(2)
index (recall that for
\mathcal{N}=4
the last factor is just
\mathbb{C}
). So fields are functions of the superspace variables of the form

V_{r_{1}\cdots\hskip 1.0ptr_{n}}(x,\theta^{m}_{\alpha},{\bar{\theta}}_{m{\dot{\alpha}}},u_{R}^{\phantom{R}m})\,.

(2.35)

The vector multiplet, in particular, has a single index and is CR-analytic

W_{r}(x,\theta^{2,3,4}_{\alpha},{\bar{\theta}}_{\alpha\,1,2,3},u)\,,\qquad\mbox{with}\qquad(D_{1}^{\phantom{1}s},\,D_{1}^{\phantom{1}4},\,D_{r}^{\phantom{r}4})W_{r}=0\,.

(2.36)

Observe that this superspace is not optimal for considering the vector multiplet as we still have 12 supercharges instead of the expected 8. Indeed we still need to impose the H-analyticity on
W
. Actually the H-analyticity follows automatically here as it will be easy to check from the next paragraph. One could argue that there are more convenient ways to study this operator, such as
(\mathcal{N},2,2)
superspace
Hartwell:1994rp
or the
\mathrm{SU}(\mathcal{N})/\mathrm{U}(1)^{3}
coset superspace
Andrianopoli:1999vr
. We will however stick with
\mathrm{p}=\mathrm{q}=1
in order to keep pursuing the comparison with
DHoker:2003csh
.

The explicit form of the bottom component of the analytic superfield can be given in terms of the more familiar
\varphi^{M}
scalars

W_{r}(x,u)\equiv u_{1}^{\phantom{1}m}u_{r+1}^{\phantom{r+1}n}\,\Sigma_{M\hskip 1.0ptmn}\,\varphi^{M}\,,\qquad r=1,2\,.

(2.37)

As we can see, it is not so different from the principle of contracting indices with polarizations. In this case only part of the
\mathrm{SU}(4)
indices are “transferred” to the
u
dependence. Consistently with the rest of our formalism, let us introduce a new
\mathrm{SU}(2)
polarization
\chi^{r}
and contract the
r
index of
W_{r}
with it

W(x,u,\chi)\equiv W_{r}(x,u)\,\chi^{r}\,.

(2.38)

In order to translate from these variables to the polarizations we are used to, we can consider the two simplest cases: the
(0,2,0)
and the
(2,0,2)
. For the former we have a totally symmetric trace

\displaystyle\Tr\hskip 1.0pt(W\,W)

\displaystyle=\chi^{r}\hskip 1.0ptu_{1}^{\phantom{1}m}u_{r+1}^{\phantom{r+1}n}\,\Sigma_{M\hskip 1.0ptmn}\;\chi^{s}\hskip 1.0ptu_{1}^{\phantom{1}p}u_{s+1}^{\phantom{s+1}q}\,\Sigma_{N\hskip 1.0ptpq}\;\Tr\hskip 1.0pt(\varphi^{M}\varphi^{N})

(2.39)

\displaystyle=y_{M}\hskip 1.0pty_{N}\,\Tr\hskip 1.0pt(\varphi^{M}\varphi^{N})\,.

It follows simply

y^{M}=\chi^{r}\hskip 1.0ptu_{1}^{\phantom{1}m}u_{r+1}^{\phantom{r+1}n}\,\Sigma^{M}_{mn}\,.

(2.40)

The case
(2,0,2)
instead requires a small computation

\displaystyle\Tr\hskip 1.0pt(W_{r}\,W_{s})\Tr\hskip 1.0pt(\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muW\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{r}\,\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muW\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{s})

\displaystyle=u_{1}^{\phantom{1}m}u_{r}^{\phantom{r}n}\,\Sigma_{M\,mn}\;u^{4}_{\phantom{1}p}u^{r}_{\phantom{r}q}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{P}^{pq}

(2.41)

\displaystyle\times u_{1}^{\phantom{1}m^{\prime}}u_{r}^{\phantom{r}n^{\prime}}\,\Sigma_{N\,m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}\;u^{4}_{\phantom{1}p^{\prime}}u^{r}_{\phantom{r}q^{\prime}}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{Q}^{p^{\prime}q^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\times\Tr\hskip 1.0pt(\varphi^{M}\varphi^{N})\Tr\hskip 1.0pt(\varphi^{P}\varphi^{Q})

\displaystyle=\eqref{Osingtrace}\,.

The matrices
u_{i}^{\phantom{i}j}
and
u^{i}_{\phantom{i}j}
are one the inverse of the other, so they yield a
\delta
of the external indices which in turn contract a pair
\Sigma,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
together. Then we use the definition
(
A.2
)
to exactly match the right hand side, provided we identify

S^{m}=4\hskip 1.0ptu_{1}^{\phantom{1}m}\,,\qquad{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{m}=4\hskip 1.0ptu^{4}_{\phantom{1}m}\,.

(2.42)

We should check that (
2.40
) and (
2.42
) are compatible with the constraints (
A.5
). This can be done very simply

\displaystyle y\cdot y

\displaystyle\propto\chi^{r}\chi^{s}\hskip 1.0ptu_{1}^{\phantom{1}m}u_{r+1}^{\phantom{r+1}n}u_{1}^{\phantom{1}p}u_{s+1}^{\phantom{s+1}q}\,\epsilon_{mnpq}\propto\epsilon_{mnpq}u_{1}^{\phantom{1}m}u_{1}^{\phantom{1}p}=0\,,

(2.43a)

\displaystyle S\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}

\displaystyle\propto u_{1}^{\phantom{1}m}u^{4}_{\phantom{1}m}=\delta^{1}_{4}=0\,.

(2.43b)

\displaystyle S{\mathrm{y}}

\displaystyle\propto u_{1}^{\phantom{1}m}\,\chi^{r}\hskip 1.0ptu_{1}^{\phantom{1}n}u_{r+1}^{\phantom{r+1}p}\epsilon_{mnpq}\propto\epsilon_{mnpq}u_{1}^{\phantom{1}m}u_{1}^{\phantom{1}n}=0\,,

(2.43c)

\displaystyle{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\bar{\mathrm{y}}

\displaystyle\propto u^{4}_{\phantom{1}m}\,\chi^{r}\hskip 1.0ptu_{1}^{\phantom{1}m}u_{r+1}^{\phantom{r+1}n}\propto\delta^{4}_{1}=0\,.

(2.43d)

So, to summarize, for our purposes going to
(\mathcal{N},1,1)
superspace is equivalent to choosing a specific parametrization of the polarizations
S,{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu},y
given by (
2.40
) and (
2.42
).

Section 3
Chiral algebra and Ward identities

3.1
Chiral algebra review

Every four-dimensional
\mathcal{N}=2
superconformal field theory admits a protected subsector which is described by a vertex operator algebra or chiral algebra. This was discovered in
Beem:2013sza
and from that seminal paper there were many developments aimed at finding similar constructions in other dimensions
Beem:2014kka
;
Chester:2014mea
;
Beem:2016cbd
and also at better understanding the structure of the chiral algebra and what it can teach us about the original superconformal field theory
Beem:2014rza
;
Beem:2017ooy
. While this story has now taken on a somewhat formal route, its source of inspiration came from a very concrete observation stemming from the results of
Nirschl:2004pa
. The seminal work
Nirschl:2004pa
provided a solution to the superconformal Ward identities for four-point functions of
\mathcal{N}=2,4
superconformal field theories. This solution is expressed in terms of a meromorphic function of a single cross-ratio
{\mathfrak{f}}(z)
, which can be obtained by evaluating the four-point function with the R-symmetry polarizations in a specific
{\bar{z}}
-dependent configuration. The function
{\mathfrak{f}}(z)
was later interpreted in
Beem:2013sza
as the four-point function in the two-dimensional chiral algebra.

We plan to apply the same idea to our correlators of interest. However, let us first quickly introduce the chiral algebra construction so that all subsequent steps in deriving the Ward identities will be clear.

The first step is to choose a two-dimensional plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}\cong\mathbb{C}
in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
along with a nilpotent supercharge
\mathbbmtt{Q}
. The supercharge is chosen in such a way that holomorphic transformations in the two-dimensional plane
\mathbb{C}
are
\mathbbmtt{Q}
-closed and the anti-holomorphic transformations are
\mathbbmtt{Q}
-exact. A concrete choice that satisfies these requirements is

\mathbbmtt{Q}=Q^{1}_{-}+\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{2\dot{-}}\,,

(3.1)

with the plane
\mathbb{C}
being
x^{1}=x^{2}=0
,
z=x^{3}+ix^{4}
and
{\bar{z}}=x^{3}-ix^{4}
. Then, once an operator
\mathcal{O}
is restricted to such plane, its
{\bar{z}}
dependence can be dropped by passing to the
\mathbbmtt{Q}
-cohomology. The operators surviving this cohomology are precisely the Schur operators mentioned around (
2.6
), located at the origin.

The key aspect of this construction is that, in order to keep the operator holomorphic when leaving the origin of the
\mathbb{C}
plane, one has to move the position in lockstep with the R-symmetry indices. This operation is known as twisted translation. In our notation it simply means that the polarization
\eta
must become
{\bar{z}}
-dependent in the following way

\chi\big[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Schur}}\big](z)\equiv\eta_{a_{1}}({\bar{z}})\cdots\eta_{a_{R}}({\bar{z}})\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Schur}}^{a_{1}\cdots a_{R}}(z,{\bar{z}})\,,\qquad\eta({\bar{z}})\equiv(1,{\bar{z}})\,.

(3.2)

The equality is understood to hold inside correlation functions, in the sense that the
{\bar{z}}
dependence on the right hand side will drop out. The resulting correlator is necessarily coupling independent because the chiral algebra is rigid under marginal deformations, thus it can be computed from the free theory.
15
15

15

In our examples we compute the protected function
\mathfrak{f}
by means of Wick contractions, made possible by the Lagrangian formulation of our theory. An alternative approach can be found in
Rastelli:2017ymc
;
Behan:2021pzk
.

In particular, we can apply the map
\chi
on four-point functions to obtain a meromorphic and protected function of a single cross ratio

\displaystyle\chi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Schur}}(x_{1},\eta_{1})\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Schur}}(x_{2},\eta_{2})\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Schur}}(x_{3},\eta_{3})\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Schur}}(x_{4},\eta_{4})\rangle\big]

(3.3)

\displaystyle\equiv\langle\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Schur}}(x_{1},\eta_{1})\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Schur}}(x_{2},\eta_{2})\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Schur}}(x_{3},\eta_{3})\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Schur}}(x_{4},\eta_{4})\rangle\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{k}^{\mu}\to{\hbox{$\left(\vbox to7.5pt{}\right.$}}0,\hskip 1.0pt0,\hskip 1.0pt\frac{z_{k}+{\bar{z}}_{k}}{2},\hskip 1.0pt\frac{z_{k}-{\bar{z}}_{k}}{2i}{\hbox{$\left)\vbox to7.5pt{}\right.$}}\\
\,\eta_{k}\hskip 1.0pt\to(1,\hskip 1.0pt{\bar{z}}_{k})\hfill\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle={\hyperlink{eqref:kinPrefz}{\mathfrak{K}_{R_{1}R_{2}R_{3}R_{4}}}}\,{\mathfrak{f}}(z)\,.

The prefactor
K
and the cross ratio
z
are defined as

{\hyperlink{eqref:kinPrefz}{\mathfrak{K}_{h_{1}h_{2}h_{3}h_{4}}}}=\frac{{\hbox{$\left(\vbox to10.0pt{}\right.$}}\frac{z_{14}}{z_{13}}{\hbox{$\left)\vbox to10.0pt{}\right.$}}^{\frac{1}{2}(h_{3}-h_{4})}{\hbox{$\left(\vbox to10.0pt{}\right.$}}\frac{z_{24}}{z_{14}}{\hbox{$\left)\vbox to10.0pt{}\right.$}}^{\frac{1}{2}(h_{1}-h_{2})}}{z_{12}^{\frac{1}{2}(h_{1}+h_{2})}\,z_{34}^{\frac{1}{2}(h_{3}+h_{4})}}\,,\qquad z=\frac{z_{12}\hskip 1.0ptz_{34}}{z_{13}\hskip 1.0ptz_{24}}=1-\frac{z_{14}\hskip 1.0ptz_{23}}{z_{13}\hskip 1.0ptz_{24}}\,,

(3.4)

where
z_{ij}=z_{i}-z_{j}
.

This construction holds for all
\mathcal{N}=2
four dimensional superconformal field theory. Therefore, in particular, it holds for
\mathcal{N}=4
super Yang-Mills. The chiral algebra of
\mathcal{N}=4
super Yang-Mills contains a Virasoro subalgebra with central charge
c_{2d}=-12\hskip 1.0ptc
where
c
is the four-dimensional central charge

c=\frac{N^{2}-1}{4}\,.

(3.5)

The Schur operators we are interested in are the superconformal primaries of the multiplets
B\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu[0\hskip 1.0pt;\hskip-0.8pt0]^{(R;\hskip 0.5pt0)}_{R}
, as we previously mentioned. Furthermore, these primaries can transform in a representation
F
of the flavor group
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. We denote them as
O_{R,F}
. Under the chiral map
\chi
, the operators
O_{R,F}
are mapped to single traces of free symplectic bosons, each being in a flavor doublet

\chi\big[O_{R,F}\big]=\tr\hskip 1.0pt(q^{a^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots q^{a^{\prime}_{R}})\,\Pi_{a_{1}\cdots a_{R}}^{b_{1}\cdots b_{F}}\,\tilde{\eta}_{b^{\prime}_{1}}\cdots\tilde{\eta}_{b^{\prime}_{F}}\,,\qquad q_{a^{\prime}}^{I}(z)\,\hskip 1.0ptq_{b^{\prime}}^{J}(0)\sim\frac{\epsilon_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}\,\delta^{IJ}}{z}\,,

(3.6)

where
\Pi_{a_{1}\cdots a_{R}}^{b_{1}\cdots b_{F}}
is a tensor responsible for contracting
R-F
indices so that the end result has
R
fields and transforms in the charge-
F
flavor representation.
16
16

16

Note that when
O_{R,F}
is taken as an
\mathcal{N}=2
sub-multiplet of an
\mathcal{N}=4
multiplet
\mathcal{O}_{p}
, one always has
R=F
. Thus, the tensor
\Pi_{a_{1}\cdots a_{R}}^{b_{1}\cdots b_{F}}
is just a product of Kronecker
\delta
’s. In the
\mathcal{O}_{0q}
instead
F=0
so the tensor is a combination of Levi-Civita tensors that contracts all the indices.
Thanks to this map, computing
{\mathfrak{f}}(z)
simply requires taking all Wick contractions among the
q
’s by using the singular OPE shown above. In order to obtain the Ward identities one has to consider different variants of the chiral algebra construction considered so far. One is obtained by exchanging the roles of
z
and
{\bar{z}}
and one is obtained by exchanging the roles of the flavor and the R-symmetry
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. Let us denote these four variants as

\chi_{z,\tilde{\eta}}\equiv\chi\,,\qquad\chi_{{\bar{z}},\tilde{\eta}}\,,\qquad\chi_{z,\eta}\,,\qquad\chi_{{\bar{z}},\eta}\,.

(3.7)

The Ward identities then read

\displaystyle\chi_{z,\tilde{\eta}}\big[\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle\big]

\displaystyle={\hyperlink{eqref:kinPrefz}{\mathfrak{K}}}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{4}\,{\mathfrak{f}}(z,\tilde{\eta})\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\chi_{{\bar{z}},\tilde{\eta}}\big[\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle\big]

\displaystyle={\hyperlink{eqref:kinPrefz}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5mu\mathfrak{K}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}}\,\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{4}\,{\mathfrak{f}}({\bar{z}},\tilde{\eta})\,,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\chi_{z,\eta}\big[\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle\big]

\displaystyle={\hyperlink{eqref:kinPrefz}{\mathfrak{K}}}\,\mathcal{P}_{4}\,{\mathfrak{f}}(z,\eta)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\chi_{{\bar{z}},\eta}\big[\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle\big]

\displaystyle={\hyperlink{eqref:kinPrefz}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5mu\mathfrak{K}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}}\,\mathcal{P}_{4}\,{\mathfrak{f}}({\bar{z}},\eta)\,,

where the function
{\mathfrak{f}}(z,\eta)
is the same for all equations,

¯

K

is the same as
K
in (
3.4
) with
z_{i}\to{\bar{z}}_{i}
and, finally,
\mathcal{P}_{4}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{4}
are kinematic prefactors defined as follows, for non-increasing
F_{i}

Aprile:2017xsp

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{4}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\eta_{12}^{\frac{1}{2}(F_{1}+F_{2}-F_{3}+F_{4})}\eta_{13}^{\frac{1}{2}(F_{1}-F_{2}+F_{3}-F_{4})}\eta_{23}^{\frac{1}{2}(-F_{1}+F_{2}+F_{3}-F_{4})}\eta_{34}^{F_{4}}&\mbox{if}\quad F_{2}+F_{3}\geq F_{1}+F_{4}\\
&\eta_{12}^{F_{2}}\hskip 1.0pt\eta_{13}^{\frac{1}{2}(F_{1}-F_{2}+F_{3}-F_{4})}\eta_{14}^{\frac{1}{2}(F_{1}-F_{2}-F_{3}+F_{4})}\eta_{34}^{\frac{1}{2}(-F_{1}+F_{2}+F_{3}+F_{4})}&\mbox{if}\quad F_{2}+F_{3}\leq F_{1}+F_{4}\end{aligned}\right.\,,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{4}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{4}\big|_{\eta_{i}\to\tilde{\eta}_{i}}\,.

Often two out of four of these identities are redundant.

3.2
Action on tensor structures

Now that we understand the construction of the chiral algebra in
\mathcal{N}=2
we can work out the concrete action of the map
\chi
directly in the
\mathcal{N}=4
tensor structures. The breaking (
2.7
) can be seen explicitly at the level of the polarizations
y^{M}
,
S^{m}
,
{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{m}
. Let us start from the six-dimensional vector: we can study the breaking in its matrix form
{\mathrm{y}}_{mn}
. The decomposition amounts to subdivide this matrix in
2\times 2
blocks. The diagonal blocks correspond to
\mathrm{U}(1)_{r}
polarizations, which we do not need, while the off-diagonal ones correspond to the two
\mathrm{SU}(2)
s. More precisely, we can write

{\mathrm{y}}_{mn}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c:c}0\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&\eta_{a}\otimes\tilde{\eta}^{b^{\prime}}\\
\hline\cr-\tilde{\eta}^{a^{\prime}}\otimes\eta_{b}\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&0\\
\end{array}\right)\,.

(3.10)

Another way to state the same equation is to take the first four components of
y^{M}
and impose

y_{A}\,\sigma^{A}_{aa^{\prime}}=\eta_{a}\tilde{\eta}_{a^{\prime}}\,.

(3.11)

One can easily check that this identification respects the constraint
y\cdot y=0
.
17
17

17

Use
\sigma_{aa^{\prime}}\cdot\sigma_{bb^{\prime}}\propto\epsilon_{ab}\epsilon_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}
together with
\eta_{a}\eta^{a}=\eta_{a}\eta_{b}\epsilon^{ab}=0
.
Similarly, we can write mappings between the
\mathrm{SU}(4)
fundamental polarizations and the
\eta,\tilde{\eta}
. This is done by splitting the components in two halves

\displaystyle S^{m}=\begin{cases}\eta^{a}&m=1,2\\
\tilde{\eta}_{a^{\prime}}&m=3,4\end{cases}\,,\qquad{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{m}=\begin{cases}\eta_{a}&m=1,2\\
\tilde{\eta}^{a^{\prime}}&m=3,4\end{cases}\,.

As before, this is compatible with
S\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=0
. Based on the decomposition (
2.8
) we must single out the charge-
p
flavor component. This is equivalent to selecting terms proportional to
\lambda^{p}_{i}
under the rescaling

\tilde{\eta}_{i}\to\lambda_{i}\hskip 1.0pt\tilde{\eta}_{i}\,.

(3.12)

In particular, if the operator under study transforms in the
(q,0,q)
, then one has to simply set
\tilde{\eta}
to zero for that operator.
To give a few examples, the simplest building blocks are decomposed as follows

\mathsf{y}_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.0pt\eta_{ij}\,\tilde{\eta}_{ij}\,,\qquad\mathsf{S}_{ij}=\eta_{ij}+\tilde{\eta}_{ij}\,.

(3.13)

Assuming that there are only
\mathcal{O}_{p}
and
\mathcal{O}_{0q}
operators, meaning that we can throw away the
\tilde{\eta}
inside
S^{m}
and
{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{m}
, the other tensor structures can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}^{ik}_{j_{1}\cdots j_{2n+1}}

\displaystyle=O(\tilde{\eta}_{i},\tilde{\eta}_{k})=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{K}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{ik}_{j_{1}\cdots j_{2n+1}}\,,

(3.14a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{J}^{ik}_{j_{1}\cdots j_{2n}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\,\eta_{i}\eta_{j_{1}}\;\tilde{\eta}_{j_{1}}\tilde{\eta}_{j_{2}}\;\eta_{j_{2}}\eta_{j_{3}}\cdots\hskip 1.0pt\tilde{\eta}_{j_{2n-1}}\hskip-0.8pt\tilde{\eta}_{j_{2n}}\;\eta_{j_{2n}}\hskip-0.8pt\eta_{k}+O(\tilde{\eta}_{i},\tilde{\eta}_{k})\,.

(3.14b)

When all operators have a nonzero
p
Dynkin label, it is possible to form cross ratios in the
y^{M}
vectors

\sigma=\frac{\mathsf{y}_{13}\hskip 1.0pt\mathsf{y}_{24}}{\mathsf{y}_{12}\hskip 1.0pt\mathsf{y}_{34}}=\alpha{\bar{\alpha}}\,,\qquad\tau=\frac{\mathsf{y}_{14}\hskip 1.0pt\mathsf{y}_{23}}{\mathsf{y}_{12}\hskip 1.0pt\mathsf{y}_{34}}=(1-\alpha)(1-{\bar{\alpha}})\,.

(3.15)

The decomposition of these cross ratios is remarkably simple as it suffices to send
\alpha
and
{\bar{\alpha}}
to the following ratios
18
18

18

The equalities of the form
\eta_{12}\hskip 1.0pt\eta_{34}+\eta_{14}\hskip 1.0pt\eta_{23}=\eta_{13}\hskip 1.0pt\eta_{24}
follow from
\epsilon_{[ab}\hskip 1.0pt\epsilon_{c]d}=0
.

\alpha=\nu\equiv\frac{\eta_{13}\hskip 1.0pt\eta_{24}}{\eta_{12}\hskip 1.0pt\eta_{34}}=1+\frac{\eta_{14}\hskip 1.0pt\eta_{23}}{\eta_{12}\hskip 1.0pt\eta_{34}}\,,\qquad{\bar{\alpha}}=\tilde{\nu}\equiv\frac{\tilde{\eta}_{13}\hskip 1.0pt\tilde{\eta}_{24}}{\tilde{\eta}_{12}\hskip 1.0pt\tilde{\eta}_{34}}=1+\frac{\tilde{\eta}_{14}\hskip 1.0pt\tilde{\eta}_{23}}{\tilde{\eta}_{12}\hskip 1.0pt\tilde{\eta}_{34}}\,.

(3.16)

When the map
\chi
is applied to the above cross ratios we get the familiar result of
Nirschl:2004pa
, namely

\chi[\nu]=\frac{1}{{\bar{z}}}\,.

(3.17)

This follows trivially from
\chi[\eta_{ij}]={\bar{z}}_{ij}
.

When considering half-BPS operators one has a further Ward identity
\mathfrak{f}(z,\tilde{\nu}=1/z)=k
with
k
a constant. This is equivalent to the topological twist of
Drukker:2009sf
. With correlators of quarter-BPS operators this property is no longer true — in our specific cases trivially so because
\mathfrak{f}
does not even depend on
\tilde{\nu}
.

3.3
Ambiguity and Ward identitites

With the methods discussed so far we are able to obtain the Ward Identities. Note that we have not proved that this represents a complete set, although for half-BPS it happens to be the case by inspection.
Let us start with any four-point function of quarter-BPS or half-BPS operators
\mathcal{G}

\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle={\hyperlink{eqref:kinPref}{\mathcal{K}}}\;\mathcal{G}(z,{\bar{z}};{\mathbf{S}}_{1},\,\ldots,\,{\mathbf{S}}_{4})\,,

(3.18)

with
K
being the kinematic prefactor defined in (
2.25
). We can expand
\mathcal{G}
in the R-symmetry tensor structures obtaining
N_{\mathrm{str}}
functions of the cross ratios

\mathcal{G}(z,{\bar{z}},{\mathbf{S}}_{1},\,\ldots,\,{\mathbf{S}}_{4})=\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{str}}}g_{k}(z,{\bar{z}})\;\mathbb{T}_{k}({\mathbf{S}}_{1},\,\ldots,\,{\mathbf{S}}_{4})\,.

(3.19)

The map
\chi
acting on the correlator will produce a function of
z
and the flavor polarizations
\tilde{\eta}

\chi\big[\langle\mathcal{O}_{1}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{3}\mathcal{O}_{4}\rangle\big]={\hyperlink{eqref:kinPrefz}{\mathfrak{K}}}\;\mathfrak{f}(z,\tilde{\eta})\,,

(3.20)

with the kinematic prefactor
\mathfrak{K}_{4}
defined in (
3.4
). Similarly, the three other variants (
3.7
) will produce their own Ward identity. This allows us to separate the functions
g_{k}(z,{\bar{z}})
into a contribution from
\mathfrak{f}(z,\tilde{\eta})
and an unprotected piece

g_{k}(z,{\bar{z}})=w_{k}(z,{\bar{z}})+\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\mathrm{u}}}\mathcal{H}_{m}(z,{\bar{z}})\;v_{k}^{(m)}(z,{\bar{z}})\,,

(3.21)

In the above decomposition, the vector
\vec{w}
contains the contribution of the chiral algebra result while the vectors
{\vec{v}^{\,(m)}=(v_{k}^{(m)})}
span the kernel of the map
\chi
in the space of tensor structures. This means that only the functions
\mathcal{H}_{m}
will contain unprotected contributions and
N_{\mathrm{u}}
denotes both the dimension of the kernel of
\chi
and the number of such unprotected functions. By definition, the above vectors must satisfy these conditions
19
19

19

Here it is understood that also the equations for the other variants hold. Here for brevity we indicate only the equations for
\chi_{z,\tilde{\eta}}
.

\displaystyle\chi\Biggl[\hskip 1.0pt\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{str}}}\mathbb{T}_{k}w_{k}\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{f}\left(z,\tilde{\eta}\right)\,,

(3.22a)

\displaystyle\chi\Biggl[\hskip 1.0pt\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{str}}}\mathbb{T}_{k}v_{k}^{(m)}\Biggr]

\displaystyle=0\,,\qquad\mbox{for}\;m=1,\,\ldots,\;\dim(\ker\chi)\equiv N_{\mathrm{u}}\,.

(3.22b)

Clearly this decomposition immediately leads to an arbitrariness given by

w_{k}(z,{\bar{z}})\;\sim\;w_{k}(z,{\bar{z}})+\sum_{m=1}^{N_{\mathrm{u}}}\mathcal{A}_{m}(z,{\bar{z}})\;v_{k}^{(m)}(z,{\bar{z}})\,.

(3.23)

Our goal is to fix the functions
\mathcal{A}_{m}(z,{\bar{z}})
as much as possible. For convenience let us introduce this terminology: the vector
\vec{w}
is called an “uplift” of the chiral algebra and the functions
\mathcal{A}_{m}(z,{\bar{z}})
are called “ambiguities.” There are a few criteria that one can use to partially fix the functional form of
\mathcal{A}_{m}(z,{\bar{z}})
.

1.

When there are degeneracies in the tensor structures, meaning that there are more
\mathbb{T}_{k}
associated to a given
\mathcal{R}
, it could be that unitarity and Bose symmetry force some of these structures to be zero.

2.

The operator of dimension two transforming in the
(0,2,0)
must be the superconformal primary
\mathcal{O}_{2}
and therefore it must appear in
\vec{w}(z,{\bar{z}})
with the same coefficient as it appears in the free theory.
20
20

20

This is because it belongs to the same multiplet as the stress tensor, whose OPE coefficient and conformal dimensions are protected. One cannot make the same reasoning for the stress tensor or the R-current: see remark before equation (
4.25
).

3.

The identity, if present, must contribute with OPE coefficient
1
as a normalization condition.

4.

The disconnected
O(N^{0})
part has to match the free theory computation.

5.

The correlator cannot exchange operators of twist two with spin higher than two due to the Maldacena-Zhiboedov theorem later extended to four dimension by Alba and Diab
Maldacena:2011jn
;
Alba:2013yda
.

In particular the last condition is very strong because the solutions to the Ward identities
\vec{w}(z,{\bar{z}})
will typically exchange operators of twist two that must disappear. Removing such contributions requires adding towers of operators in other R-symmetry structures in order to keep the full ambiguity within the kernel of
\chi
.

Let us end with a few additional comments about these criteria: first of all, these requirements solve any possible issue related to multiplet recombination involving the Konishi operator. However for twist greater than two, these are no longer enough to achieve such a recombination.

A second important remark regards whether or not these criteria fully incorporate all the conditions dictated by unitarity of superconformal representations. As a general rule, the answer is not: since we are expanding in usual conformal blocks, all the exchanged primary operators manifestly satisfy the unitarity constraints just for the conformal group. Exploiting the full power of superconformal symmetry would require using superconformal blocks, which are known only for correlators of all half-BPS operators. Superblocks organize the expansion in term of superprimaries, this allows really to tell if a given operator is below unitarity, in the full superconformal sense, and hence must be canceled. Quite surprisingly, it turns out that for
\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
criteria 1–5 are equivalent to the one imposed by the full superconformal symmetry since they completely fix the ambiguity.

3.4
A familiar example revisited

In order to get acquainted with this slightly more general point of view for imposing Ward identities, let us revisit a familiar example: the four-point function of the half-BPS operator
\mathcal{O}_{2}
.
In this case the chiral algebra result
\mathfrak{f}
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{f}}(z,\tilde{\nu})

\displaystyle=\hat{{\mathfrak{f}}}(z,\tilde{\nu})+\hat{{\mathfrak{f}}}\Big(\frac{z}{z-1},1-\tilde{\nu}\Big)\,,\qquad\hat{{\mathfrak{f}}}(z,\tilde{\nu})\equiv\frac{1}{32}+\frac{\tilde{\nu}^{2}\hskip 1.0ptz^{2}}{16}+\frac{\tilde{\nu}(2-\tilde{\nu})\hskip 1.0ptz}{4\hskip 1.0pt(N^{2}-1)}\,.

(3.24)

There are six tensor structures given by the first six polynomials in equation (B.14) of
Nirschl:2004pa
, to give a few:

\mathbb{T}_{1}=1\,,\quad\ldots\,,\quad\mathbb{T}_{6}=\sigma^{2}+\tau^{2}+4\hskip 0.5pt\sigma\tau-\frac{4}{5}(\sigma+\tau)+\frac{1}{10}\,.

(3.25)

If we try to uplift the function
\mathfrak{f}
to the full correlator we find that there is just one ambiguity degree of freedom, consistent with the fact that we expect only one
\mathcal{H}(z,{\bar{z}})
function. The ambiguity
\mathcal{A}
in this case is obvious: it is just a redefinition of
\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{A}
.

In the well known derivation of the partial wave decomposition
Dolan:2004iy
, the function
\mathcal{A}(z,{\bar{z}})
appears as a consequence of the fact that the chiral algebra result needs to be properly uplifted in order to get the protected contribution to the four-point function. In other words: the correlator always admits a splitting as a constant, a one-variable degree of freedom and a two-variables degree of freedom. The chiral algebra twist fixes the constant and the one-variable function. However, the protected spectrum contributes to the two-variable function as well. The precise way in which this happens can be derived from the knowledge of the superconformal blocks of short multiplets. Since we will not have this knowledge for the cases of quarter-BPS operators, our goal for the rest of this section is to obtain the same result as Dolan and Osborn without using the form of the superconformal blocks. We will indeed see that criteria 1–5 of section
3.3
are sufficient for this.

Following the definitions above, the vectors
\vec{w}
and
\vec{v}^{\hskip 1.0pt(1)}
can be chosen as

\displaystyle\vec{w}

\displaystyle=\big\{*,\ldots,*,0\big\}\,,

(3.26)

\displaystyle\vec{v}^{\hskip 1.0pt(1)}

\displaystyle=\big\{\tfrac{3z^{2}{\bar{z}}^{2}-12z^{2}{\bar{z}}+10z^{2}-12z{\bar{z}}^{2}+64z{\bar{z}}-60z+10{\bar{z}}^{2}-60{\bar{z}}+60}{60z^{2}{\bar{z}}^{2}},

\displaystyle\tfrac{(z-2)({\bar{z}}-2)(z{\bar{z}}-z-{\bar{z}})}{4z^{2}{\bar{z}}^{2}},\tfrac{6z^{2}{\bar{z}}^{2}-15z^{2}{\bar{z}}+10z^{2}-15z{\bar{z}}^{2}+10z{\bar{z}}+10{\bar{z}}^{2}}{60z^{2}{\bar{z}}^{2}},

\displaystyle\tfrac{2z{\bar{z}}-3z-3{\bar{z}}+6}{6z{\bar{z}}},\tfrac{z{\bar{z}}-z-{\bar{z}}}{2z{\bar{z}}},\tfrac{1}{6}\big\}\,,

where in place of the
*
’s there are some functions of
\mathfrak{f}(z,\tilde{\nu})
,
\mathfrak{f}(z,\nu)
,
\mathfrak{f}({\bar{z}},\tilde{\nu})
and
\mathfrak{f}({\bar{z}},\nu)
that we will not specify here for brevity. For concreteness, one can check that the four-point function comes out as expected, namely

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{6}\mathbb{T}_{k}\Big(w_{k}(z,{\bar{z}})+\mathcal{H}(z,{\bar{z}})\,v_{k}^{(1)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\alpha z-1)(\bar{\alpha}{\bar{z}}-1)\,\mathfrak{f}(z,\bar{\alpha})+(\alpha\leftrightarrow\bar{\alpha}\;\mathrm{or}\;z\leftrightarrow{\bar{z}})}{(z-{\bar{z}})(\bar{\alpha}-\alpha)}

(3.27)

\displaystyle+\frac{(\alpha z-1)(\alpha{\bar{z}}-1)(\bar{\alpha}z-1)(\bar{\alpha}{\bar{z}}-1)}{z^{2}{\bar{z}}^{2}}\mathcal{H}(z,{\bar{z}})\,.

The fact that this choice satisfies (
3.22
) is readily verified by noticing that here
\chi
is simply the replacement
\alpha\to 1/{\bar{z}},\,\bar{\alpha}\to\tilde{\nu}
.
21
21

21

We obviously need to match also all other chiral algebra limits, i.e.
\bar{\alpha}\to 1/z,\alpha\to 1/z
and
\bar{\alpha}\to 1/{\bar{z}}
. Here for simplicity of notation we indicated only one of them.

Now, the vector
\vec{w}
defined as in (
3.26
) contains higher spin twist twos and twist zeros in all nonzero five entries. We can cancel them by adding various higher twist contributions in the ambiguity
\mathcal{A}(z,{\bar{z}})
, which will contribute to the correlator as in (
3.23
) with
N_{\mathrm{u}}=1
. These will appear in the other entries with lower twist thanks to the recursion relations written in appendix
E.1
. For example, a single block
g_{6,2}
in
\mathcal{A}(z,{\bar{z}})
will contribute to the representation
(1,2,1)
— structure
\mathbb{T}_{5}
— as follows

\mathcal{A}(z,{\bar{z}})=g_{6,2}(z,{\bar{z}})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle\big|_{(1,2,1)}=\tfrac{3}{2}\hskip 1.0ptg_{5,1}+6\hskip 1.0ptg_{5,3}+\tfrac{1}{8}\hskip 1.0ptg_{7,1}+\tfrac{8}{21}\hskip 1.0ptg_{7,3}\,.

(3.28)

The other entries are more involved but they can be obtained with repeated applications of the relations in appendix
E.1
. Notice that in the above example a twist-four contribution was able to generate a twist-two contribution in a different representation, as advertised. In order to do this more systematically we can take an ansatz for
\mathcal{A}(z,{\bar{z}})
made of an infinite sum of conformal blocks
22
22

22

The convention that we use for conformal blocks is given in appendix
A.2
.
of twist four and six with arbitrary coefficients

\mathcal{A}(z,{\bar{z}})=\sum_{\tau=4,6}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}a_{\tau+\ell,\ell}\,g_{\tau+\ell,\ell}(z,{\bar{z}})\,.

(3.29)

Then we truncate this sum up to a maximal spin
\ell_{\mathrm{max}}
and impose that the twist two and zero up to
\ell_{\mathrm{max}}
cancel. Remarkably, the solution is unique for each
\ell_{\mathrm{max}}
. This will not be true for quarter-BPS operators or for higher half-BPS operators
\mathcal{O}_{p}
. We can then easily spot a pattern and extend this solution to the non-truncated equation. The coefficients that enter in
\mathcal{A}(z,{\bar{z}})
are the ones found in (3.11) of
Dolan:2004iy
, modulo some factors due to the conventions. For convenience we report them here

\displaystyle a_{\ell+4,\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{\ell}((\ell+1)!)^{2}\,\big((\ell+1)(\ell+2)+\frac{1}{c}\big)}{3(2\ell+2)!}\,,

(3.30a)

\displaystyle a_{\ell+6,\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{\ell}((\ell+2)!)^{2}\,\big((\ell+1)(\ell+4)-\frac{3}{c}\big)}{6(2\ell+4)!}\,.

(3.30b)

After resumming these coefficients with their blocks, which can be done following the steps described in appendix
E.2
, we reproduce exactly the known result

\mathcal{A}(z,{\bar{z}})=\frac{1}{6}\,z^{2}{\bar{z}}^{2}\,\big(\hskip 1.0pt\mbox{equation (2.31) of \cite[cite]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{Beem:2016wfs}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}}\,\big)\,.

(3.31)

The overall factor with respect to
Beem:2016wfs
is due to a different choice of normalization and conventions. By explicit inspection we see that

\sum_{k=1}^{6}\mathbb{T}_{k}\Big(w_{k}(z,{\bar{z}})+\mathcal{A}(z,{\bar{z}})\,v_{k}^{(1)}\Big)

(3.32)

is free of twist zero and twist two operators. We can therefore take
\mathcal{H}(z,{\bar{z}})
as having only unprotected contributions with anomalous dimensions. Note that in this computation we did not use at all the form of the superconformal blocks. In fact, it was not even needed. In the subsequent cases having the superconformal blocks would instead be very beneficial but, unfortunately, the blocks for external quarter-BPS operators are not known yet. We therefore have no choice but using this method, which, as we will see, is still able to produce some useful results.

Section 4
Cases of interest

In this section we introduce the quarter-BPS operator transforming in the (2,0,2) SU(4) representation, which will be at the center of our investigation. We proceed by discussing in details various four-point functions including this operator. We report the results for correlator with one, two or four
\mathcal{O}_{02}
’s. The case
\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
is not very illuminating: there are no constraints coming from the ambiguity resolution discussed in subsection
3.3
and it does not provide new information about unknown OPE data. Thus, although we analyzed it, we decided not to include this example in the main discussion.

4.1
The
\boldsymbol{(2,0,2)}
quarter-BPS operator

Let us define in detail the quarter-BPS operator transforming in the
(2,0,2)
in terms of free fields. It turns out that we need to take a specific linear combination if we want to end up with a quarter-BPS operator in the interacting theory as well. As before, we can write both a single trace and a double trace version

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{02}^{(\mathrm{st},1)}(x,{\mathbf{S}})=\tr\left(\varphi^{M_{1}}\varphi^{M_{2}}\varphi^{M_{3}}\varphi^{M_{4}}\right)\,S\cdot\Sigma_{M_{1}M_{2}}\hskip-0.8pt\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\;S\cdot\Sigma_{M_{3}M_{4}}\hskip-0.8pt\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,,

(4.1a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{02}^{(\mathrm{st},2)}(x,{\mathbf{S}})=\tr\left(\varphi^{M_{1}}\varphi^{M_{2}}\varphi^{M_{3}}\varphi^{M_{4}}\right)\,S\cdot\Sigma_{M_{1}M_{3}}\hskip-0.8pt\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\;S\cdot\Sigma_{M_{2}M_{4}}\hskip-0.8pt\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,.

(4.1b)

In the single trace case there are, a priori, two possibilities. Whereas for double trace we find

\mathcal{O}_{02}^{(\mathrm{dt})}(x,{\mathbf{S}})=\tr\left(\varphi^{M_{1}}\varphi^{M_{2}}\right)\,\tr\left(\varphi^{M_{3}}\varphi^{M_{4}}\right)\,S\cdot\Sigma_{M_{1}M_{3}}\hskip-0.8pt\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\;S\cdot\Sigma_{M_{2}M_{4}}\hskip-0.8pt\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,.

(4.2)

If we compute the two-point function of
\mathcal{O}^{(\mathrm{st},2)}_{02}
we notice that it vanishes so the operator must be identically zero, as explicitly shown in appendix
C
. Then
Ryzhov:2001bp
;
DHoker:2003csh
teach us that the correct linear combination that remains protected is
23
23

23

Note that this and the following expressions in the present section are exact in
N
.

\mathcal{O}_{02}^{(\mathrm{dt})}(x,{\mathbf{S}})+\frac{2}{N}\,\mathcal{O}_{02}^{(\mathrm{st},1)}(x,{\mathbf{S}})\,.

(4.3)

This is also consistent with the fact that at large
N
the single trace operators disappear. The orthogonal combination to this one, namely
\mathcal{O}_{02}^{(\mathrm{st},1)}
, is actually not a superconformal primary. It is the
Q^{2}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}{}^{2}
superdescendant of the free Konishi operator as we will show in subsection
7.3
. As it is well known, this operator will be lifted by quantum corrections in the interacting theory.

We want to define our operator such that its two-point function is given by (
2.19
), with
p=0
and
q={\bar{q}}=2
. A simple computation shows that the correct normalization is the following

\mathcal{O}_{02}(x,{\mathbf{S}})=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{3}\sqrt{(N^{2}-4)(N^{2}-1)}}\,\left(\mathcal{O}_{02}^{(\mathrm{dt})}(x,{\mathbf{S}})+\frac{2}{N}\,\mathcal{O}_{02}^{(\mathrm{st},1)}(x,{\mathbf{S}})\right)\,.

(4.4)

For completeness, let us also give the normalized
\mathcal{O}_{2}
operator

\mathcal{O}_{2}(x,y)=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{N^{2}-1}}\,\tr\hskip 1.0pt(\varphi\cdot y)^{2}\,.

(4.5)

4.2
