We would like to thank Xinan Zhou for valuable comments on the draft. AM would like to thank Leonardo Rastelli for discussions. This work is supported by Knut and Alice Wallenberg Foundation under grant KAW 2016.0129 and by VR grant 2018-04438.
Some computations in this work were enabled by resources in project SNIC 2020/15-320 provided by the Swedish National Infrastructure for Computing (SNIC) at UPPMAX, partially funded by the Swedish Research Council through grant agreement no. 2018-05973.

Appendix A
Notation and conventions

A.1
Notation for R-symmetry structures

In this appendix we will show in detail the conventions used for tensor structures and polarizations. In table
9
we summarize our naming convention for the various labels and indices that appear throughout the manuscript.

The tensor structures in four dimensions are a function of a six-dimensional complex vector
y
and two four-dimensional complex twistors
S
and
{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
. The tensor structures in the chiral algebra are instead functions of two spinors
\eta
and
\tilde{\eta}
associated to the R-symmetry and flavor
\mathrm{SU}(2)
respectively. Since
y
has to contract symmetric traceless tensors, it is subject to the constraints
y\cdot y=0
. Similarly, if we want to describe tensors of
\mathrm{SU}(4)
with
S^{m}
and
{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{m}
, we need to remove the traces by imposing
S\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=0
. The polarizations
\eta,\tilde{\eta}
do not need other constraints because they automatically square to zero.
40
40

40

See footnote
17
.

Let us first discuss the R-symmetry structures in four dimensions. Here follows our convention for the six dimensional Clifford algebra

\displaystyle\Sigma^{A}_{mn}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c:c}0\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&-\sigma^{A}\hskip 1.0pt\hat{\epsilon}\\
\hline\cr{\bar{\sigma}}^{A}\hskip 1.0pt\check{\epsilon}\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&0\\
\end{array}\right)\,,\quad\Sigma^{5}_{mn}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c:c}-i\hat{\epsilon}\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&0\\
\hline\cr 0\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&i\hat{\epsilon}\\
\end{array}\right)\,,\quad\Sigma^{6}_{mn}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c:c}\hat{\epsilon}\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&0\\
\hline\cr 0\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&\hat{\epsilon}\\
\end{array}\right)\,.

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{A}^{mn}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c:c}0\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&-\hat{\epsilon}\hskip 1.0pt\sigma^{A}\\
\hline\cr\check{\epsilon}\hskip 1.0pt{\bar{\sigma}}^{A}\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&0\\
\end{array}\right)\,,\quad\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{5}^{mn}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c:c}-i\hat{\epsilon}\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&0\\
\hline\cr 0\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&i\hat{\epsilon}\\
\end{array}\right)\,,\quad\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{6}^{mn}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c:c}-\hat{\epsilon}\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&0\\
\hline\cr 0\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@add@cssclass&-\hat{\epsilon}\\
\end{array}\right)\,,

where
\sigma^{A=1,2,3}_{aa^{\prime}}
are Pauli matrices,
\sigma^{4}_{aa^{\prime}}=i\hskip 1.0pt\delta_{aa^{\prime}}
,
{\bar{\sigma}}^{A}=-(\hat{\epsilon}\sigma^{A}\hat{\epsilon})^{T}
,
\hat{\epsilon}=\epsilon^{ab}=-\epsilon_{ab}=\check{\epsilon}
with
\epsilon^{12}=1
.
With this definition we can build the generators of
\mathrm{SO}(6)

\displaystyle\bigl(\Sigma^{MN}\bigr)_{m}^{\phantom{m}n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(\Sigma^{M}_{mp}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{N\hskip 1.0ptpn}-\Sigma^{N}_{mp}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{M\hskip 1.0ptpn}\right)\,,

(A.2)

\displaystyle\bigl(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{MN}\bigr)^{m}_{\phantom{m}n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{M\hskip 1.0ptmp}\,\Sigma^{N}_{pn}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{N\hskip 1.0ptmp}\,\Sigma^{M}_{pn}\right)\,.

These matrices can also be used to relate tensors in the adjoint of
\mathrm{SU}(4)
with tensors in the rank-two antisymmetric of
\mathrm{SO}(6)
. We can also map
\mathrm{SO}(6)
fundamental indices to antisymmetric
\mathrm{SU}(4)
indices as follows

{\mathrm{y}}_{mn}\equiv y_{M}\Sigma^{M}_{mn}\,,\qquad\bar{\mathrm{y}}^{mn}\equiv y_{M}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{M\hskip 1.0ptmn}\,.

(A.3)

Some useful identities of the
\Sigma
matrices are

\displaystyle\Sigma^{M}_{mn}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{N\,np}+\Sigma^{N}_{mn}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}{}^{M\,np}

\displaystyle=\delta^{p}_{m}\delta^{MN}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\epsilon^{mnpq}\,\Sigma^{M}_{pq}

\displaystyle=-2{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}{}^{M\,mn}

(A.4a)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{P}_{mn}\Sigma_{P\,pq}

\displaystyle=2\epsilon_{mnpq}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\Sigma^{P}_{mn}{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\Sigma\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}_{P}^{pq}

\displaystyle=2\bigl(\delta^{q}_{m}\delta^{p}_{n}-\delta^{p}_{m}\delta^{q}_{n}\bigr)\,.

(A.4b)

If we have operators in the
(q,p,q)
where all Dynkin labels are nonzero the conditions on the polarizations stated at the beginning are not enough and we need to impose further constraints. The complete list is the following

y\cdot y=0\,,\qquad S\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=0\,,\qquad{\mathrm{y}}S=0\,,\qquad\bar{\mathrm{y}}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=0\,.

(A.5)

As explained around (
2.16
), operators in the
(q,p,q)
will be fields with homogeneity
p
in
y
and
q
in
S,{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}

\mathcal{O}_{pq}(\lambda S,\bar{\lambda}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu},\kappa y)=(\lambda\bar{\lambda})^{q}\kappa^{p}\,\mathcal{O}_{pq}(S,{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu},y)\,.

(A.6)

In order to recover the tensor form of this operator we must differentiate with respect to the polarizations. However, we need to be careful because the polarizations are constrained and so their derivatives are not free. The following differential operators can be used to avoid this problem
Bargmann:1977gy
;
Nirschl:2004pa
;
Costa:2011mg
;
Cuomo:2017wme

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{M}

\displaystyle=\left(2+y\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{M}}-\frac{1}{2}y_{M}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y\cdot\partial y}\,,

(A.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{m}

\displaystyle=\left(3+S\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial S}+{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial S^{m}}-{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{m}\,\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial S\cdot\partial{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}}\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}^{m}

\displaystyle=\left(3+S\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial S}+{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{m}}-S^{m}\,\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial S\cdot\partial{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}}\,.

With the aid of the above operators we would like to write

(\mathcal{O}_{pq})^{m_{1}\cdots m_{q}}_{n_{1}\cdots n_{q}\,,\,M_{1}\cdots M_{p}}=\partial_{n_{1}}\cdots\partial_{n_{q}}\,\bar{\partial}^{m_{1}}\cdots\bar{\partial}^{m_{q}}\,\mathcal{D}_{M_{1}}\cdots\mathcal{D}_{M_{p}}\mathcal{O}_{pq}(S,{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu},y)\,.

(A.8)

However, unfortunately, the operators do not implement the constraints
{\mathrm{y}}S={\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\bar{\mathrm{y}}=0
and so the derivatives are not free and the above expression does not hold unless either
p
or
q
is zero.

The conventions for
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{R}\times\mathrm{SU}(2)_{F}
are analogous to the usual spinor notation in four dimensions
WessnBagger
with the substitutions
a\to\alpha,\,a^{\prime}\to{\dot{\alpha}},\,A\to\mu
and the Euclidean signature.
An operator in the
(R\,;F)
will be a field with homogeneity
R
in
\eta
and
F
in
\tilde{\eta}

O_{R,F}(\lambda\eta,\tilde{\lambda}\tilde{\eta})=\lambda^{R}\tilde{\lambda}^{F}\,O_{R,F}(\eta,\tilde{\eta})\,.

(A.9)

Since there are no constraints on
\eta
and
\tilde{\eta}
, in order to recover the indices one simply needs to take derivatives

(O_{R,F})_{a_{1}\cdots a_{R}\,,\,a^{\prime}_{1}\cdots a^{\prime}_{F}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta^{a_{1}}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial\eta^{a_{R}}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\eta}^{a^{\prime}_{1}}}\cdots\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\eta}^{a^{\prime}_{F}}}\,O_{R,F}(\eta,\tilde{\eta})\,.

A.2
Convention for the conformal blocks

The OPE coefficients that we show in the text are meaningful only if we specify the normalization of the conformal blocks. The definition that we use is the following

g_{\Delta,\ell}(z,{\bar{z}})=g_{\Delta,\ell}^{0,0}(z,{\bar{z}})\,,

(A.10)

g^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}_{\Delta,\ell}(z,{\bar{z}})=\frac{z{\bar{z}}}{z-{\bar{z}}}\frac{1}{(-2)^{\ell}}\left(\kappa_{\Delta+\ell}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}(z)\kappa_{\Delta-\ell-2}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}({\bar{z}})-(z\leftrightarrow{\bar{z}})\right)\,,

(A.11)

with

\kappa_{\beta}^{a,b}(z)=z^{\beta/2}\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{\beta-a}{2},\frac{\beta+b}{2};\beta;z\right)\,.

(A.12)

Appendix B
Ward identities for the
\boldsymbol{(2,0,2)}
four-point function

Here we show the nonzero entries of the vector
\hat{v}_{k}^{(m)}(z,{\bar{z}})
introduced in (
4.43
) in terms of seven polynomials
P_{1}
,
\ldots
,
P_{7}
which we will define below

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{41}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{112P_{6}P_{1}\hskip-0.8pt[1]}{15z^{3}{\bar{z}}^{3}}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{42}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{5}}{7560z^{3}{\bar{z}}^{3}}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{41}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{112P_{2}}{9z^{2}{\bar{z}}^{2}}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{42}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{P_{7}P_{1}\hskip-0.8pt[1]}{6z^{2}{\bar{z}}^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{41}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{56P_{2}}{9z^{2}{\bar{z}}^{2}}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{42}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{P_{7}P_{1}\hskip-0.8pt[1]}{12z^{2}{\bar{z}}^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{41}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=\frac{16P_{1}\hskip-0.8pt[1]}{z{\bar{z}}}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{42}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=\frac{P_{1}\hskip-0.8pt\big[\tfrac{2}{3}\big]+2}{42z{\bar{z}}}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{v}_{41}^{(5)}

Appendix C
Proof of vanishing of low-lying operators

In section
2
we argued that the simplest quarter-BPS operator of
B\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
type is
\mathcal{O}_{02}
. In this appendix we show why it is impossible to construct
\mathcal{O}_{11}
and
\mathcal{O}_{21}
and why
\mathcal{O}^{(\mathrm{st},2)}_{02}
in (
4.1b
) vanishes as well.

Let us start from the first two. Both of these operators must be written solely out of the six scalars in
\varphi^{M}
. The latter case has potentially two options: it could be of single trace type

\mathcal{O}_{21}^{(\mathrm{st})}(S,{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu},y)=\tr\hskip 1.0pt\left(\varphi^{M_{1}}\varphi^{M_{2}}\varphi^{M_{3}}\varphi^{M_{4}}\right)\,S\cdot\Sigma_{M_{1}M_{4}}\hskip-0.8pt\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,\hskip 1.0pty_{M_{2}}y_{M_{3}}\,,

(C.1)

or the analogous one with
(M_{1}M_{3})

(M_{2}M_{4})
pairing. Or it could be of double trace type

\mathcal{O}_{21}^{(\mathrm{dt})}(S,{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu},y)=\tr\hskip 1.0pt\left(\varphi^{M_{1}}\varphi^{M_{2}})\,\tr\hskip 1.0pt(\varphi^{M_{3}}\varphi^{M_{4}}\right)\,S\cdot\Sigma_{M_{1}M_{4}}\hskip-0.8pt\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,\hskip 1.0pty_{M_{2}}y_{M_{3}}\,.

(C.2)

The second trivially vanishes as can be shown by a
(14)(23)
permutation, recalling that the
\Sigma_{M_{1}M_{4}}
are antisymmetric. The first also vanishes in
\mathrm{SU}(N)
due to the fact that the generators are Hermitian and the trace of four of them is real, which implies

\tr\hskip 1.0pt(T^{I_{1}}T^{I_{2}}T^{I_{3}}T^{I_{4}})=\tr\hskip 1.0pt(T^{I_{4}}T^{I_{3}}T^{I_{2}}T^{I_{1}})\,.

(C.3)

The former case instead can only be single trace since it has three fields

\mathcal{O}_{11}=\tr\hskip 1.0pt\left(\varphi^{M_{1}}\varphi^{M_{2}}\varphi^{M_{3}}\right)\,S\cdot\Sigma_{M_{1}M_{3}}\hskip-0.8pt\cdot{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,\hskip 1.0pty_{M_{2}}\,.

(C.4)

This operator would seem allowed, however a quick computation shows that its two-point function identically vanishes and thus the operator must be absent in a unitary theory.

We will now show why the single trace operator in (
4.1b
) actually vanishes. For simplicity let us rewrite here is explicit form

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{(\mathrm{st},2)}_{02}=\varphi^{M_{1}}_{a}\varphi^{M_{2}}_{b}\varphi^{M_{3}}_{c}\varphi^{M_{4}}_{d}\,\tr\hskip 1.0pt(T^{a}T^{b}T^{c}T^{d})\,S\cdot\Sigma_{M_{1}M_{3}}\cdot\bar{S}S\cdot\Sigma_{M_{2}M_{4}}\cdot\bar{S}\,.

(C.5)

The SU(
N
) generators satisfy two important identities

\displaystyle f^{abe}f^{cde}=\frac{2}{N}(\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}-\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc})+d^{ace}d^{bde}-d^{ade}d^{bce}\,,

(C.6)

\displaystyle f^{abe}d^{cde}+f^{cbe}d^{dae}+f^{dbe}d^{ace}=0\,,

(C.7)

where
d^{abc}
is a totally symmetric tensor and
f^{abc}
is the completely antisymmetric structure constant. By using these relations one can write

\displaystyle\text{tr}(T^{a}T^{b}T^{c}T^{d})=\frac{\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}}{4N}+\frac{1}{8}(d^{abe}d^{cde}+i\,d^{cde}f^{abe}+i\,d^{abe}f^{cde}-\underbrace{f^{abe}f^{cde}}_{\text{using \eqref{Identity1}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}-\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}+\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc}}{4N}+\frac{1}{8}(d^{abe}d^{cde}-d^{ace}d^{dbe}+d^{ade}d^{bce})+\frac{i}{8}(d^{abe}f^{cde}+\underbrace{d^{cde}f^{abe}}_{\text{using \eqref{Identity2}}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta^{ab}\delta^{cd}-\delta^{ac}\delta^{bd}+\delta^{ad}\delta^{bc}}{4N}+\frac{1}{8}(d^{abe}d^{cde}-d^{ace}d^{dbe}+d^{ade}d^{bce})

\displaystyle\;\quad+\frac{i}{8}(d^{abe}f^{cde}-d^{ace}f^{dbe}+d^{ade}f^{bce})

(C.8)

Now that we have rewritten the trace appearing in
\mathcal{O}^{(\mathrm{st},2)}_{02}
in this way, it is straightforward to argue that given the antisymmetry under
M_{2}\leftrightarrow M_{4}
(or equivalently for
M_{1}\leftrightarrow M_{3}
) the first two terms in (
C.8
), being symmetric, they vanish. The last piece, instead, is antisymmetric, so the same argument can not be applied. By using (
C.7
) it is possible to rewrite this term of the trace as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} d^{bae}f^{dce}-d^{bde}f^{cae}+d^{bce}f^{ade}&=-d^{abe}f^{cde}-d^{bde}f^{cae}+d^{bce}f^{ade}\\
&=-d^{abe}f^{cde}-(f^{bce}d^{dae}+f^{dce}d^{abe})-(f^{cde}d^{bae}+f^{bde}d^{ace})\\
&=-d^{abe}f^{cde}+d^{ace}f^{dbe}-d^{ade}f^{bce}\,,\end{aligned}

(C.9)

which is clearly antisymmetric under
a\leftrightarrow b
together with
c\leftrightarrow d
. However under the corresponding simultaneous exchange of
(1,2)(3,4)
the
\mathrm{SO}(6)
part is symmetric, so finally their product vanishes.

Appendix D
Useful integrals for computing the inversion formula

In this appendix we collect all the relevant computations necessary to obtain the OPE coefficients and anomalous dimensions in section
6
.

Let us start from the
{\bar{z}}
integral in (
5.2b
). Since in the studied examples we have at most
\log(1-{\bar{z}})
, which has vanishing double discontinuity, we will only focus on

\overline{\mathcal{I}}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d{\bar{z}}}{{\bar{z}}^{2}}(1-{\bar{z}})^{\frac{\Delta_{34}-\Delta_{12}}{2}}\kappa_{2h+2\ell}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}({\bar{z}})\hskip 1.0pt{\bar{z}}^{-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}}\text{dDisc}\left[\left(\frac{1-{\bar{z}}}{{\bar{z}}}\right)^{\lambda}\right]\,,

(D.1)

where we recall that we have defined
h
as half the twist
2h=\tau=\Delta-\ell
.
This integral can be performed by replacing the hypergeometric function in
\kappa_{2h+2\ell}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}({\bar{z}})
with its integral transform by introducing a fictitious integration variable
V
and then by performing the change of variables
{\bar{z}}\to\frac{T}{(T-1)V+1}
. The result is

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{I}}_{\lambda}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\Gamma(2(h+\ell))\Gamma\left(h+\ell-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}-\lambda-1\right)}{\Gamma\left(h+\ell-\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(h+\ell-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(h+\ell+\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}+\lambda+1\right)}\times

\displaystyle\;\quad\times\sin(\pi\lambda)\Gamma(\lambda+1)\sin\left(\pi\frac{\Delta_{34}-\Delta_{12}}{2}+\pi\lambda\right)\,\Gamma\left(-\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2}+\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}+\lambda+1\right)

(D.2)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi^{2}\Gamma(2h+2\ell)\Gamma\left(h+\ell-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}-\lambda-1\right)}{\Gamma(-\lambda)\Gamma\left(\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2}-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}-\lambda\right)\Gamma\left(h+\ell-\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(h+\ell-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(h+\ell+\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}+\lambda+1\right)}\,,

where in the second line we have assumed
\lambda<0
and we have used Euler’s reflection formula
\Gamma(1-x)\Gamma(x)=\pi/\sin(\pi x)
. Notice that the
\Gamma
functions that appear are regular for all the relevant values of
\lambda
and
\Delta_{i}
. Translated to
c_{k}(\Delta,\ell)
this means that the
{\bar{z}}
integral just provides the spin dependence of the OPE data that we want to extract and does not give us any information about the twist of the possible exchange operators.

The information we are after is instead encoded in the
z
integrals. These can be distinguished in three different types

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{z^{2}}(1-z)^{\frac{\Delta_{34}-\Delta_{12}}{2}}\kappa_{4-2h}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}(z)\left[\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{\lambda}z^{-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}}\right]\,,

(D.3a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{z^{2}}(1-z)^{\frac{\Delta_{34}-\Delta_{12}}{2}}\kappa_{4-2h}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}(z)\hskip 1.0pt\left[z^{\lambda}z^{-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}}\right]\,,

(D.3b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{3}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dz}{z^{2}}(1-z)^{\frac{\Delta_{34}-\Delta_{12}}{2}}\kappa_{4-2h}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}(z)\left[\log z\left(\frac{z}{1-z}\right)^{\lambda}z^{-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}}\right]\,.

(D.3c)

In all these cases
\lambda
has to be considered positive and we are assuming
h\in\mathbb{N},\,h\geq 1
.
41
41

41

When solving these integrals spurious poles can appear at half integer values of
h
. We will ignore them having in mind that they can be cancelled by adding a reflected block with
h\to 1-h

Caron-Huot:2017vep
;
Alday:2017vkk
.
The first integral can be computed similarly to the
{\bar{z}}
one and gives

\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}(\lambda)=-\frac{\pi\hskip 1.0ptr^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}_{h,\lambda}\sin\left(\pi\left(\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2}+h\right)\right)\sin\left(\pi\left(\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}+h\right)\right)\sin\left(\pi\left(-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}+h+\lambda\right)\right)}{\sin(2\pi h)\sin(\pi\lambda)\sin\left(\pi\left(\frac{\Delta_{12}-\Delta_{34}}{2}+\lambda\right)\right)\sin\left(\pi\left(\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}+h-\lambda\right)\right)}\,,

(D.4)

where we have collected ratios of
\Gamma
functions appearing throughout these computations in a single function

r^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}_{h,\lambda}=\frac{\Gamma\left(h+\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2}-1\right)\Gamma\left(h+\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}-1\right)\Gamma\left(h-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}+\lambda-2\right)}{\Gamma(2h-3)\Gamma(\lambda)\Gamma\left(\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2}-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}+\lambda\right)\Gamma\left(h+\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}-\lambda\right)}\,.

(D.5)

We are now going to analyze in detail the poles that
\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}(\lambda)
develops and the corresponding residues for all the values of the external dimensions appearing in the main text.

•

\mathbf{\Delta_{12}=\Delta_{34}=0}
: we have simple poles for
h=\lambda+1+n
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
with residues

\text{Res}_{h=\lambda+1+n}\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{I}_{1}^{0,0}(\lambda)=-r^{0,0}_{\lambda+n+1,\lambda}\,.

(D.6)

•

\mathbf{\Delta_{12}=-\Delta_{34}=-2}
: we have simple poles for
h=\lambda+n
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
with residues

\text{Res}_{h=\lambda+n}\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{I}_{1}^{-2,2}(\lambda)=-r^{-2,2}_{\lambda+n,\lambda}\,.

(D.7)

•

\mathbf{\Delta_{12}=\Delta_{34}=2}
: we have simple poles for
h=\lambda+1+n
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
with residues

\text{Res}_{h=\lambda+n}\hskip 1.0pt\mathcal{I}_{1}^{2,2}(\lambda)=-r^{2,2}_{\lambda+n,\lambda}\,.

(D.8)

There is a small caveat in this case: the function
r_{h,\lambda}^{2,2}
is not well defined for
\lambda=1
at
h=1,\,2
. More generally it is not possible to properly define the residue of the corresponding integral for these specific values. To overcome this problem, in the main text, when computing the contributions from
\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
to
\langle a^{(0)}_{k,\Delta,\ell}\rangle
, we have used the usual expansion in conformal blocks. These results seem to suggest that
\mathrm{Res}_{h=1,2}\mathcal{I}_{1}^{2,2}(1)=0
, as well as
\mathrm{Res}_{h=2}\mathcal{I}_{1}^{2,2}(2)=0
.

Moving to the second kind of integral, the integration can now be performed by replacing the hypergeoemetric function contained in
\kappa_{4-2h}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}
with its series representation. By resumming the results after integration we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{2}^{\Delta_{12},\Delta_{34}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}(-\Delta_{12}+\Delta_{34}+2)\right)\Gamma\left(-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}-h+\lambda+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(-\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2}-h+\lambda+2\right)}\times

(D.9)

\displaystyle\times{}_{3}F_{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2}-h+2\,,\quad\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}-h+2\,,\quad-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}-h+\lambda+1\\
4-2h\,,\quad-\frac{\Delta_{12}}{2}-h+\lambda+2\end{array};1\right)\,.

By using some identities for the generalized hypergeometric function it is easy to study the residues associated to this integral for the interesting values of the external dimensions.

•

\boldsymbol{\Delta_{12}=\Delta_{34}=0}
: we have simple poles for
h=\lambda+n+1,\,n\in\mathbb{N}
with residues

\mathrm{Res}_{h=\lambda+n+1}\mathcal{I}_{2}^{0,0}(\lambda)=(\scalebox{0.75}[1.0]{$-$}1)^{n+1}r_{\lambda+n+1,\lambda}^{0,0}\,.

(D.10)

•

\boldsymbol{\Delta_{12}=\Delta_{34}=2}
: we have simple poles for
h=\lambda+n\,n\in\mathbb{N}
with residues

\mathrm{Res}_{h=\lambda+n}\mathcal{I}_{2}^{2,2}(\lambda)=\frac{(\lambda-2)(\lambda-1)(-1)^{n+1}}{(\lambda+n-2)(\lambda+n-1)}r_{\lambda+n,\lambda}^{2,2}\,.

(D.11)

Notice that the residue vanishes for
\lambda=1,\,2
for any twist.

The last integral, the one containing
\log z
, is the hardest one and it was not possible to find a closed formula valid for any value of
\Delta_{12}
and
\Delta_{34}
. Thus we will report only the results necessary to reproduce the computations of the main discussion.

•

\boldsymbol{\Delta_{12}=\Delta_{34}=0}
: for
\lambda>0
and
h\geq 1

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{0,0}_{3}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi^{2}r_{h,\lambda}^{0,0}}{\sin^{2}(\pi(\lambda-h))}+\frac{\pi\tan(\pi h)\sin(\pi(h+\lambda)))r_{h,\lambda}}{2\sin^{2}(\pi\lambda)\sin(\pi(\lambda-h)}\Big(H_{h-\lambda}+H_{\lambda+h}

(D.12)

\displaystyle-2H_{h}-\frac{1}{\lambda+h-2}-\frac{1}{\lambda+h-1}-\frac{1}{\lambda+h}+\frac{1}{\lambda-h}+\frac{2}{h-1}+\frac{2}{h}\Big)\,,

where
H_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}1/k
is the
n
-th harmonic number, or rather its analytic continuation to the complex plane.

•

\boldsymbol{-\Delta_{12}=\Delta_{34}\in\mathbb{N}>0}
: for
\lambda>0
and
h\geq 1

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{-\Delta_{34},\Delta_{34}}_{3}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{-\pi^{2}r_{h,\lambda}^{-\Delta_{34},\Delta_{34}}}{\sin^{2}\left(\pi\left(\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}-\lambda+h\right)\right)}+\Pi^{(\Delta_{34})}_{h,\lambda}r_{h,\lambda}^{-\Delta_{34},\Delta_{34}}\Big(-\frac{2}{\Delta_{34}-2\lambda+2h}

(D.13)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha=\pm 1}\left(\frac{2}{2h+\alpha\Delta_{34}-2}+\frac{2}{2h-\alpha\Delta_{34}}\right)+\sum_{k=0}^{2}\frac{2}{\Delta_{34}-2(-k+\lambda+h)}

\displaystyle+H_{\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}-\lambda+h}+H_{-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}+\lambda+h}-H_{h-\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}}-H_{\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}+h}\Big)\,,

where we have defined

\Pi^{(\Delta_{34})}_{h,\lambda}=\frac{\pi\sin\left(\frac{1}{2}\pi(\Delta_{34}-2h)\right)\sin\left(\frac{1}{2}\pi(\Delta_{34}+2h)\right)\sin\left(\frac{1}{2}\pi(\Delta_{34}-2(\lambda+h))\right)}{\sin(\pi\lambda)\sin(2\pi h)\sin(\pi(\Delta_{34}-\lambda))\sin\left(\pi\left(\frac{\Delta_{34}}{2}-\lambda+h\right)\right)}\,.

(D.14)

In both cases notice the appearance of double poles: these are the signs of anomalous dimensions.

Appendix E
Useful tools for resolving the ambiguity

E.1
Recursion relations for conformal blocks

In subsection
3.3
we explained how to fix part of the ambiguity by imposing that some twist-two operators vanish. The way in which this happens in practice is that a twist-four addition to the ambiguity functions
\mathcal{A}_{m}(z,{\bar{z}})
can result into twist-two and higher contributions to the correlator. This is a consequence of the entries of
v_{k}^{(m)}(z,{\bar{z}})
which can multiply the blocks and shift their dimension. An explicit example that shows how this comes about was given in equation (
3.28
).

All these contributions with shifted twist can be obtained thanks to the recursion relations satisfied by the conformal blocks. The special case with equal external dimensions was given in
Dolan:2001tt
. For a general scalar conformal block in four dimensions one has the following recursion relations

\displaystyle\frac{1+v}{u}g_{\Delta,\ell}^{(a,b)}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} &-\frac{1}{2}\hskip 1.0ptg_{\Delta-1,\ell-1}^{(a,b)}-2\hskip 1.0ptg_{\Delta-1,\ell+1}^{(a,b)}-\frac{\big(J^{2}-a^{2}\big)\big(J^{2}-b^{2}\big)}{8(J-1)J^{2}(J+1)}\,g_{\Delta+1,\ell+1}^{(a,b)}\\
&-\frac{\big((\tau-2)^{2}-a^{2}\big)\big((\tau-2)^{2}-b^{2}\big)}{32(\tau-1)(\tau-2)^{2}(\tau-3)}\,g_{\Delta+1,\ell-1}^{(a,b)}+\\
&+\frac{ab\left((\Delta-2)^{2}+\ell(\ell+2)\right)}{(\tau-2)(\tau-4)(J-2)J}\,g_{\Delta,\ell}^{(a,b)}\end{aligned}

(E.1a)

\displaystyle\frac{1-v}{u}g_{\Delta,\ell}^{(a,b)}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} &2\hskip 1.0ptg_{\Delta-2,\ell}^{(a,b)}-\frac{ab\left(a^{2}-J^{2}\right)\left(J^{2}-b^{2}\right)}{8(J-1)J^{2}(J+1)(2-\tau)(4-\tau)}\,g_{\Delta+1,\ell+1}^{(a,b)}+\\
&+\frac{\left(J^{2}-a^{2}\right)\left(J^{2}-b^{2}\right)}{2(J-1)J^{2}(J+1)}\,g_{\Delta,\ell+2}^{(a,b)}+\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{a^{2}b^{2}}{(J-2)J(\tau-2)(\tau-4)}+1\right)g_{\Delta,\ell}^{(a,b)}+\\
&-\frac{ab\left((\tau-2)^{2}-a^{2}\right)\left((\tau-2)^{2}-b^{2}\right)}{32(J-2)J(\tau-1)(\tau-2)^{2}(\tau-3)}\,g_{\Delta+1,\ell-1}^{(a,b)}+\\
&+\frac{\left((\tau-2)^{2}-a^{2}\right)\left((\tau-2)^{2}-b^{2}\right)}{32(\tau-1)(\tau-2)^{2}(\tau-3)}\,g_{\Delta,\ell-2}^{(a,b)}+\\
&+\frac{\left((\tau-2)^{2}-a^{2}\right)\left((\tau-2)^{2}-b^{2}\right)(J^{2}-b^{2})(J^{2}-a^{2})}{128(J-1)J^{2}(J+1)(\tau-1)(\tau-2)^{2}(\tau-3)}\,g_{\Delta+2,\ell}^{(a,b)}+\\
&-\frac{2ab}{(J-2)J}\,g_{\Delta-1,\ell+1}^{(a,b)}-\frac{ab}{2(\tau-2)(\tau-4)}\,g_{\Delta-1,\ell-1}^{(a,b)}\end{aligned}

(E.1b)

where
a=\Delta_{12}
,
b=\Delta_{34}
,
\tau=\Delta-\ell
,
J=\Delta+\ell
and
u=z{\bar{z}}
,
v=(1-z)(1-{\bar{z}})
. These two relations are sufficient for all cases considered here.

E.2
Resumming blocks with coefficients

The ambiguity resolution for the correlators
\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
and
\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{02}\rangle
involves an infinite family of conformal blocks of fixed twist

\mathcal{A}_{m}(z,{\bar{z}})=\sum_{\ell=\ell_{0}}^{\infty}\,a_{\tau+\ell,\ell}\,g_{\tau+\ell,\ell}(z,{\bar{z}})\,,

(E.2)

with some given coefficients
a_{\tau+\ell,\ell}
and fixed integer
\tau
. The goal of this appendix is to show how to perform this type of sums. First we separate the
z
and the
{\bar{z}}
dependent parts by multiplying the sum by
z-{\bar{z}}

(z-{\bar{z}})\mathcal{A}_{m}(z,{\bar{z}})=z{\bar{z}}\,\kappa_{\tau-2}({\bar{z}})\sum_{\ell=\ell_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{a_{\tau+\ell,\ell}}{(-2)^{\ell}}\hskip 1.0pt\kappa_{2\ell+\tau}(z)-(z\leftrightarrow{\bar{z}})\,,

(E.3)

Next we use the integral representation for the hypergeometric function inside
\kappa_{2\ell+\tau}(z)
. Under the assumption that the coefficients are suppressed enough to make the series convergent, we can swap the sum and the integral signs to obtain

S(z,t)=\frac{t^{\frac{\tau+b-1}{2}}(1-t)^{\frac{\tau-b-1}{2}}}{(1-tz)^{\frac{\tau-a}{2}}}\,\sum_{\ell=\ell_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{a_{\tau+\ell,\ell}}{(-2)^{\ell}}\hskip 1.0pt\frac{\Gamma(2\ell+\tau)}{\Gamma\left(\ell+\frac{\tau+b}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\ell+\frac{\tau-b}{2}\right)}\,\left(\frac{t(1-t)}{1-tz}\right)^{\ell}\,,

(E.4)

with
a=\Delta_{12}
and
b=\Delta_{34}
. If the coefficients
a_{\tau+\ell,\ell}
come from a free theory OPE, we expect their expressions to involve ratios of
\Gamma
functions and polynomials in
\ell
, possibly with some
(-1)^{\ell}
. If that is the case, the sum
S(z,t)
can be performed in terms of hypergeometric functions. When
\tau
is an integer, such hypergeometric functions will reduce to rational functions. Then we only need to perform the final integration in
\mathrm{d}t
over the interval
[0,1]
.

\mathcal{A}_{m}(z,{\bar{z}})=\frac{z{\bar{z}}}{z-{\bar{z}}}\,\kappa_{\tau-2}({\bar{z}})\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\,S(z,t)+(z\leftrightarrow{\bar{z}})\,.

(E.5)

For this purpose, it is convenient to make the change of variables
t=\frac{s-z}{(s-1)z}
which makes the integral somewhat easier to perform.

Appendix F
\boldsymbol{D}
-functions

It is possible to strip a kinematic prefactor from the
D
-functions defined in (
7.5
) so as to obtain a function of the cross ratios
z,{\bar{z}}
only. Furthermore we can also remove the pole in
\Sigma=2
that renders
D_{1111}
ill defined. This leads to a “reduced”
D
-function denoted as
{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
. The standard definition is the following

{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}(z,{\bar{z}})=\frac{(x_{13}^{2})^{\Sigma-\Delta_{4}}(x_{24}^{2})^{\Delta_{2}}}{(x_{14}^{2})^{\Sigma-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{4}}(x_{34}^{2})^{\Sigma-\Delta_{3}-\Delta_{4}}}\frac{2\hskip 1.0pt\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Gamma(\Delta_{i})}{\pi^{2}\hskip 1.0pt\Gamma(\Sigma-2)}\,D_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{4})\,.

(F.1)

The recursion relations (
7.21
) can be written as derivatives with respect to
z
and
{\bar{z}}
of this reduced function as follows
Arutyunov:2002fh
;
Rastelli:2017udc

\displaystyle{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}+1\,\Delta_{2}+1\,\Delta_{3}\,\Delta_{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-z)\partial_{z}-(1-{\bar{z}})\partial_{\bar{z}}}{z-{\bar{z}}}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}\,,

\displaystyle{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{3}+1\,\Delta_{4}+1}

\displaystyle=\left(z{\bar{z}}\,\frac{(1-z)\partial_{z}-(1-{\bar{z}})\partial_{\bar{z}}}{z-{\bar{z}}}+\Sigma-\Delta_{1}-\Delta_{2}\right){\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}\,,

\displaystyle{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}\,\Delta_{2}+1\,\Delta_{3}+1\,\Delta_{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{z\partial_{z}-{\bar{z}}\partial_{\bar{z}}}{z-{\bar{z}}}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}\,,

\displaystyle{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}+1\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{3}\,\Delta_{4}+1}

\displaystyle=\left((1-z)(1-{\bar{z}})\,\frac{z\partial_{z}-{\bar{z}}\partial_{\bar{z}}}{z-{\bar{z}}}+\Sigma-\Delta_{2}-\Delta_{3}\right){\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}\,,

\displaystyle{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}\,\Delta_{2}+1\,\Delta_{3}\,\Delta_{4}+1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{z(z-1)\partial_{z}-{\bar{z}}({\bar{z}}-1)\partial_{\bar{z}}}{z-{\bar{z}}}+\Delta_{2}\right){\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}\,,

\displaystyle{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}+1\,\Delta_{2}\,\Delta_{3}+1\,\Delta_{4}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{z(z-1)\partial_{z}-{\bar{z}}({\bar{z}}-1)\partial_{\bar{z}}}{z-{\bar{z}}}+\Sigma-\Delta_{4}\right){\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}\,.

(F.2)

with
\Sigma=\frac{1}{2}(\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{2}+\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4})
.
The seed
{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{1111}
is known in closed form and it reads

{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{1111}(z,{\bar{z}})=\frac{1}{z-{\bar{z}}}\left(2\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)-2\hskip 1.0pt\mathrm{Li}_{2}({\bar{z}})+\log(z{\bar{z}})\,\log\!{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.09}{\hbox{$\left(\vbox to13.5pt{}\right.$}}}}}\frac{1-z}{1-{\bar{z}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.09}{\hbox{$\left)\vbox to13.5pt{}\right.$}}}}}\right)\,.

(F.3)

The
{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
-functions satisfy the same permutation identities as conformal four-point functions. Namely

\displaystyle{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}(z,{\bar{z}})

\displaystyle=\big((1-z)(1-{\bar{z}})\big)^{-\Delta_{2}}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{4}\Delta_{3}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.09}{\hbox{$\left(\vbox to13.5pt{}\right.$}}}}}\frac{z}{z-1},\frac{{\bar{z}}}{{\bar{z}}-1}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.09}{\hbox{$\left)\vbox to13.5pt{}\right.$}}}}}\,,

(F.4)

\displaystyle=\big((1-z)(1-{\bar{z}})\big)^{\Delta_{4}-\Sigma}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{2}\Delta_{1}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.09}{\hbox{$\left(\vbox to13.5pt{}\right.$}}}}}\frac{z}{z-1},\frac{{\bar{z}}}{{\bar{z}}-1}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{1.09}{\hbox{$\left)\vbox to13.5pt{}\right.$}}}}}\,,

\displaystyle=\big((1-z)(1-{\bar{z}})\big)^{\Delta_{1}+\Delta_{4}-\Sigma}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{2}\Delta_{1}\Delta_{4}\Delta_{3}}(z,{\bar{z}})\,,

\displaystyle={\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{3}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{1}\Delta_{4}}(1-z,1-{\bar{z}})\,,

\displaystyle=(z{\bar{z}})^{\Delta_{3}+\Delta_{4}-\Sigma}{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{4}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{1}}(z,{\bar{z}})\,.

They also satisfy the following identity
Dolan:2004iy

{\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Delta_{1}\Delta_{2}\Delta_{3}\Delta_{4}}(z{\bar{z}})={\mkern 3.5mu\overline{\mkern-3.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}_{\Sigma-\Delta_{3}\,\Sigma-\Delta_{4}\,\Sigma-\Delta_{1}\,\Sigma-\Delta_{2}}(z{\bar{z}})\,.

(F.5)

Appendix G
Some comments on
\boldsymbol{\langle\mathcal{O}_{0q}\mathcal{O}_{0q}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle}

In Section
4.3
we have discussed the correlator
\langle\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{02}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
and we have enumerated all the possible representations exchanged, as reported in table
3
, and the corresponding tensor structures
T

k

II

. If now one considers a similar four-point function but with a generic quarter-BPS operator
\mathcal{O}_{0q}
, as expected there is a proliferation of exchanged representations
42
42

42

In order to find this expression we did explicitly the cases for
q=1,\ldots 7
with
LieART
and tried to extrapolate a reasonable pattern. We also checked that the dimensions agree for many values of
q
.

\displaystyle(q,0,q)\otimes(q,0,q)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\delta=-q}^{q}\bigoplus_{n=0}^{2q-2|\delta|}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=|\delta|\\
m\equiv\delta\;\mathrm{mod}\;2\end{subarray}}^{2q-n-|\delta|}\,\mu_{n,m}^{(\delta)}\,(n+\delta+|\delta|,m,n-\delta+|\delta|)\,,

(G.1)

\displaystyle\mu_{n,m}^{(\delta)}

\displaystyle=\min(n+1,2q-|\delta|-n-m+1)\,.

However, when considering the representations appearing in the intersection with
(0,2,0)\otimes(0,2,0)
, (
G.1
) reduces to the ones in table
3
with the exact same multiplicities. This observation tells us that the tensor structures in
\langle\mathcal{O}_{0q}\mathcal{O}_{0q}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
should be related to the ones found for the
q=2
case and indeed we obtain
43
43

43

Notice that this is not true for
\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{0q}\mathcal{O}_{0q}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
.

\mathbb{T}^{\langle\mathcal{O}_{0q}\mathcal{O}_{0q}\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle}_{k}=(\mathsf{S}_{12}\mathsf{S}_{21})^{q-2}\hskip 1.0pt{\hyperlink{eqref:TQQHHDef}{\mathbb{T}_{k}^{\mathrm{\hskip 0.5ptII}}}}\,.

(G.2)

Given the simplicity of the tensor structures a similar analysis as the one for the
\mathcal{O}_{02}
case can be performed in this channel and we leave this to future works. Extending our results to
\langle\mathcal{O}_{2}\mathcal{O}_{0q}\mathcal{O}_{0q}\mathcal{O}_{2}\rangle
and eventually to
\langle\mathcal{O}_{0q}\mathcal{O}_{0q}\mathcal{O}_{0q}\mathcal{O}_{0q}\rangle
seems instead way harder to achieve and would require a more efficient way of dealing with the increasing number of representations exchanged.
